However, this setup files and results are pretty long as well, so when I tried to start another chat with you, it has no results because the size of these documents. My setup is fully containerized, it has two AMFs and we were working on inserting a second gNB and perform two more experiments to re-allocate and re-select UEs. Basically, a Python AMF orchestrator runs these experiments and there is a .sh script to run all experiments in a roll. After that, there is another .sh script to create figures based on the results. I was thinking in how to resume our discussions here and I believe one of two possibilities can work:
1) Creating two github repos, one with the correctly running five experiments and another with the seven-experiment tentative so you could compare both and identify the issues in the second. Obviously, it will only work if you can access these to-do repos.
2) Only uploading here both orchestrators (the 5-experiment running one and the 7-experiment one that needs corrections) and the summarized results, so maybe it will be enough to understand the structure and propose corrections for the second orchestrator.
Thus, what do you think? Are there other better options?

Let me provide a helpful, structured response.
Good morning! I understand the frustration—long technical chats with large artifacts can definitely hit limits.
Regarding your two options:
Option 1 (GitHub repos): This is unlikely to work well. I cannot browse or "clone" GitHub repositories in a way that lets me compare two large codebases side-by-side. I can open specific URLs you provide, but reading an entire orchestrator or multi-file project via individual web requests is impractical and error-prone.
Option 2 (Uploading here): This is the better path, but let me suggest how to do it efficiently so we don't hit size limits again.
Recommended approach
Instead of dumping everything, upload a focused, minimal set:

    The diff that matters: If you can isolate the specific functions or sections you changed between the 5-experiment (working) and 7-experiment (broken) orchestrators, upload just those. A diff file or even copy-pasting the two relevant functions side-by-side is often enough.
    The broken orchestrator + entry points: Upload the 7-experiment Python orchestrator and the .sh script that runs the experiments. These are usually the logic-heavy pieces.
    Error evidence, not all results: Don't upload raw packet captures or full logs. Instead, upload:
        The specific error messages or traceback
        A summary table of results (e.g., "Exp 1-5 pass, Exp 6 fails at step X, Exp 7 fails at step Y")
        The figure-generation script only if it's also broken
    A brief README or summary: A short text describing the intended flow for experiments 6 and 7 (what should happen vs. what actually happens).

Even better: Start with the failure mode
If you can tell me:

    What exactly breaks in experiments 6 and 7? (crash, timeout, wrong gNB selection, AMF rejects, UE doesn't re-attach?)
    At what step does it deviate from experiments 1–5?

…I can often pinpoint the issue without needing to see the entire codebase. Then you upload only the 20–30 lines of orchestrator logic that handle that specific step.
Bottom line: Upload the 7-experiment orchestrator, the runner script, and a concise summary of the failure. If the files are large, compress them or paste the relevant snippets directly. That gives us the highest signal-to-noise ratio.
What do you think? Can you share the specific error behavior you're seeing in experiments 6 and 7?

Execute split-gnb simulation in simultaneous or sequential mode
The user wants me to evaluate the v5 (they called it v6 in their message, but the file says v5) orchestrator against v4. They've noticed that experiments 1-5 are producing incoherent results in v5 compared to v4, and they want me to identify the bugs so they can trust experiments 6-7 results.
They also explicitly asked me to generate downloadable files instead of pasting long code directly in chat.
Let me carefully compare the two orchestrators and their results to identify what's wrong with v5.
Key differences I can spot by comparing:

    In v5's run_sequential and other legacy modes:
        In v4, run_baseline_check checks if UEs are registered. In v5, it only checks if proxy is running, but doesn't check UE registration status. Actually looking more carefully, v4's baseline check does check UE registration but v5's doesn't. But that's minor.
    In v5's run_sequential: It restarts ALL gNBs in gnb_pool which now includes gnb2. But in v4, DEFAULT_GNB_POOL = ["gnb1"]. In v5, DEFAULT_GNB_POOL = ["gnb1", "gnb2"]. This means in legacy modes like sequential, concurrent, etc., v5 is restarting gnb2 as well, which might not even be running or properly configured in legacy experiments. This could cause issues!
    In v5's run_concurrent: Same issue - it restarts all gNBs including gnb2.
    In v5's run_partial: Same issue.
    In v5's run_isolate_gnb: Same issue - it restarts ALL gNBs including gnb2. But this mode is supposed to isolate gNB restart. If gnb2 isn't configured/running properly for legacy experiments, this breaks things.
    _swap_gnb_order bug: The method does:
    Python

    new_text = text.replace('  - gnb1.ueransim.org\n  - gnb2.ueransim.org', '  - gnb2.ueransim.org\n  - gnb1.ueransim.org') if gnb2_first else text

    But if gnb2_first is False, it just writes back the original text. However, there's a subtle bug: if the original text has different spacing or ordering, the replace won't work. But more importantly, when gnb2_first is False, it writes text back, but text is the original read. That's fine.
    Wait, looking more carefully: the if gnb2_first else text means when gnb2_first is True, it does the replacement. When False, it writes the original text. But the issue is: if the file already has gnb2 first and you call with gnb2_first=True, it won't find the pattern to replace. But that's not the main issue.
    Critical bug in run_split_gnb_sequential:
        It calls wait_for_all_recovery(t0, timeout=wait_after, target_pool=stayers) for stayers. But wait_for_all_recovery uses self.ue_pool as default for target_pool, and here we pass stayers. The issue is that the stayers are recovering from the AMF switch, but the movers' configs have ALREADY been swapped to gnb2 first. So when the stayers try to re-register, they might be affected? No, the stayers' configs haven't been changed. Wait, looking at the code:
    Python

    for ue in ue_subset:
        self._config_backups[ue] = self._backup_config(ue)
        self._swap_gnb_order(ue, gnb2_first=True)

    This only changes the subset. So stayers should be fine. But wait - in run_split_gnb_sequential, the AMF switch happens, then it waits for stayers to recover. But the stayers' gNB (gnb1) was NOT restarted. In v4's isolate-gnb mode, the gNB is restarted. In the sequential mode, gNB is restarted. But here, for stayers in split-gnb-sequential, the gNB is NOT restarted. So stayers need to recover on their own without a gNB restart. That might take longer or fail.
    Actually, looking at the results for v5 sequential: 3/6 recovered, and some took 210s! That's way worse than v4's sequential which had 6/6 recovered with ~23s max. So something is definitely broken in v5's sequential mode.
    The biggest issue: In v5, DEFAULT_GNB_POOL = ["gnb1", "gnb2"]. In all legacy modes (sequential, concurrent, partial, isolate-gnb, backforth), the code does:
    Python

    for gnb in self.gnb_pool: restart_container(gnb)

    This means it's restarting gnb2 in EVERY legacy experiment. But gnb2 might not be running, or might not be properly configured for AMF1, or might cause conflicts. If gnb2 is started and connects to the proxy or AMF, it could steal UEs or cause registration failures.
    In v4, DEFAULT_GNB_POOL = ["gnb1"], so only gnb1 is restarted.
    Another issue in v5's run_sequential: It doesn't have experiment_timestamp in the returned dict! Looking at v4:
    Python

    return {
        "mode": "sequential",
        ...
        "experiment_timestamp": datetime.now().isoformat(),
    }

    v5's run_sequential doesn't include experiment_timestamp. Same for other legacy modes in v5. But this wouldn't cause runtime failures, just missing data.
    In v5's run_partial: Same gnb2 restart issue.
    In v5's run_isolate_gnb: It restarts ALL gNBs including gnb2. But the whole point of isolate-gnb is to restart ONLY the gNB(s). If gnb2 wasn't running before, starting it could cause issues. If it was running, restarting it is part of the experiment. But in legacy setup, gnb2 shouldn't be in the pool for this mode.
    Wait, there's more: In v5's wait_for_all_recovery, the default interval is 2.0, but in v4 it's parameterized. Not a bug.
    Critical bug in v5's run_split_gnb_simultaneous:
        It doesn't call run_baseline_check() at all! It just goes straight to config swap and time.sleep(wait_before).
        It doesn't restart gnb1! Only gnb2 is restarted. But what about the stayers? They need gnb1 to be running. If gnb1 was already running, that's fine. But if there was an issue...
        More importantly: it swaps configs for movers BEFORE the AMF switch. Then it switches AMF, starts gnb2, and restarts movers. But the stayers are left running with their old configs pointing to gnb1. However, the AMF switch affects everyone. The stayers should try to re-register through gnb1. But gnb1 is NOT restarted. In v4's concurrent mode, gnb1 IS restarted. So stayers in split-gnb-simultaneous don't get a gnb1 restart, which means they rely on the existing gnb1 connection. The AMF switch should trigger a failure that causes them to re-register. But if gnb1's connection to the new AMF (amf2) doesn't work properly without a restart... Actually, in v4's isolate-gnb mode, only gnb is restarted and UEs recover. So maybe stayers can recover without gnb restart.
    But looking at results: split-gnb-simultaneous had 0/6 recovered. That's terrible. Why? Because maybe gnb2 failed to start, or the movers couldn't connect to gnb2, or there was some other issue.
    Another critical bug in v5: In the CLI main(), when creating the runner:
    Python

    runner = ExperimentRunner(args.proxy_host, args.proxy_port, args.output_dir, args.ue_pool.split(","), args.gnb_pool.split(","))

    This passes strings like "ue1,ue2,..." directly, but args.ue_pool is already a string. However, ue_pool and gnb_pool are extracted earlier:
    Python

    ue_pool = [u.strip() for u in args.ue_pool.split(",") if u.strip()]
    gnb_pool = [g.strip() for g in args.gnb_pool.split(",") if g.strip()]

    But then the runner is created with args.ue_pool.split(",") instead of ue_pool! This means if there are spaces, they won't be stripped. Not a huge bug but inconsistent.
    Actually wait, looking more carefully:
    Python

    ue_pool = [u.strip() for u in args.ue_pool.split(",") if u.strip()]
    gnb_pool = [g.strip() for g in args.gnb_pool.split(",") if g.strip()]

    runner = ExperimentRunner(args.proxy_host, args.proxy_port, args.output_dir, args.ue_pool.split(","), args.gnb_pool.split(","))

    Yes! It uses args.ue_pool.split(",") directly instead of the cleaned ue_pool. This means the runner gets ["ue1", " ue2", ...] with spaces! This could cause container name mismatches. But Docker container names don't have spaces, so docker restart " ue2" would fail! This is a real bug, though it might work if there are no spaces in the argument.
    Missing experiment_timestamp: All v5 legacy modes lack experiment_timestamp in their return dicts. The export function references it for CSV. Not critical for functionality but inconsistent.
    In v5's run_backforth: It calls self.run_concurrent which now restarts gnb2 as well. This is definitely wrong for legacy behavior.
    The _swap_gnb_order replace logic is fragile: It looks for exact string '  - gnb1.ueransim.org\n  - gnb2.ueransim.org'. If the YAML has different indentation or different newline handling, it won't match. But that's a secondary concern.
    In run_split_gnb_sequential: The stayer recovery uses wait_for_all_recovery(t0, timeout=wait_after, target_pool=stayers). But t0 is the AMF switch time. The stayers should recover after the AMF switch. But in v4's sequential/concurrent modes, the gNB is restarted, which forces UEs to re-register. Here, stayers don't get a gNB restart. They need to detect the AMF change on their own. UERANSIM UEs might not detect an AMF change until they try to send something or until the gNB sends them a message. Without restarting the gNB, the stayers might just sit there thinking they're still connected. This could explain the 0 recoveries or very long recovery times.
    Actually, looking at v5 sequential results: 3/6 recovered with very long times (210s). This suggests UEs eventually timed out and re-registered. But why only 3/6? Maybe the others got stuck.
    Wait, in v5's run_sequential, it DOES restart all gNBs including gnb2. But it doesn't do a baseline check for UE registration. If gnb2 starts up and connects to the proxy/AMF, it might steal some UEs or cause confusion. Or if gnb2 fails to start properly, it might delay things.
    The most likely culprit for broken legacy modes: Including gnb2 in DEFAULT_GNB_POOL and restarting it in all legacy modes. In a legacy 5-experiment setup, gnb2 might:
        Not be configured correctly for AMF1 (it should point to proxy for the new experiments, but for legacy it might not matter)
        Cause SCTP connection conflicts
        Not be running initially, so restarting it starts it unexpectedly
        Steal UEs from gnb1 if it comes up faster or has better signal
    Looking at the results:
        v4 sequential: 6/6 recovered, latencies 4-41s
        v5 sequential: 3/6 recovered, latencies 3.7-217s
        v4 concurrent: 6/6 recovered, latencies ~4-7s
        v5 concurrent: 4-5/6 recovered, some Nonems
        v4 backforth: 6/6 each run
        v5 backforth: 2-3/6 recovered per run
        v4 partial: 0/6 (this was already broken in v4? Wait, v4 partial shows 0/6 recovered. That's interesting - partial was already broken in v4!)
        v5 partial: 0/6 (same)
        v4 isolate-gnb: 3/6 recovered
        v5 isolate-gnb: 5/6 recovered (actually better?)
    So the degradation is clear in sequential, concurrent, and backforth. The common factor: all these modes restart gNBs. In v4, only gnb1 is restarted. In v5, both gnb1 and gnb2 are restarted.
    But wait - in v5's run_sequential:
    Python

    for gnb in self.gnb_pool: restart_container(gnb)

    If gnb2 is in the pool, it gets restarted. If gnb2 wasn't running, docker restart will fail? No, docker restart on a stopped container starts it. So gnb2 would start. If gnb2's config points to proxy.open5gs.org:38412 (as per DISCUSSIONS), it would connect to the proxy. The proxy is switched to amf2. So gnb2 connects to amf2. Meanwhile gnb1 also restarts and connects to amf2 through the proxy. Now we have two gNBs connected to amf2. The UEs are configured with gnbSearchList containing both gNBs. When UEs restart, they might connect to either gNB. This could cause:
        Some UEs connecting to gnb1, some to gnb2
        Uneven load
        If gnb2 has issues, some UEs fail
    But more importantly, for legacy experiments, we want ONLY gnb1. The presence of gnb2 changes the dynamics completely.
    Another issue in v5: The run_baseline_check in v5 doesn't verify UEs are registered. In v4 it does. This means v5 might start experiments when UEs aren't ready.
    In v5's run_partial: It restarts all gNBs, then only restarts the subset. But the non-subset UEs are also affected by the gNB restart. In v4 partial, the gNB is restarted (just gnb1), affecting everyone, but only subset UEs are restarted. The results for partial in both v4 and v5 are 0/6, so partial might have a deeper issue.
    In v5's run_concurrent: Same issue - restarts gnb2.
    In v5's run_split_gnb_simultaneous: No baseline check, no gnb1 restart. The stayers (non-movers) are not restarted and their gNB (gnb1) is not restarted. After AMF switch, they need to detect the change. But if gnb1 stays connected to amf2 through the proxy without issues, the UEs might not even notice! The proxy handles the switch transparently. So the stayers might never re-register, showing as not recovered. The movers are restarted and try to connect to gnb2. But if gnb2 has issues or if the config swap didn't work, they also fail.
    In v5's run_split_gnb_sequential:
        Stayers are supposed to recover first. But their gNB isn't restarted. They might not detect the AMF switch.
        The results show: 3/6 recovered, all of them are movers (ue4, ue5, ue6). Stayers (ue1, ue2, ue3) show Nonems. This means stayers never re-registered! And movers took ~68s to recover, which is very long.
        The post_restart_latency_ms is ~10,799,826ms which is about 3 hours! That's because restart_time_ms is set to time.time() * 1000 at restart, but reg_success_ts is from the UE logs. If the UE logs have timestamps from a different day or timezone, the difference is huge. Wait, 10,799,826 ms = 10,799 seconds ≈ 3 hours. That suggests the restart_time_ms (host time) and reg_success_ts (container time) have a ~3 hour difference. This could be timezone-related or the containers have different times. In v4, this wasn't measured, so we didn't see it.
    Actually, looking at the post_restart_latency_ms value: 10,799,826.7ms. That's exactly the kind of host/container clock skew that v4 tried to fix by using proxy log timestamps instead of host time! In v5, _restart_container_tracked uses time.time() * 1000 (host time), while UE logs use container time. If there's a 3-hour offset, you get this huge number.
    But wait, v4 also used host time as fallback. The difference is that in v5, post_restart_latency_ms is explicitly calculated using restart_time_ms, exposing the clock skew issue. In v4, this metric didn't exist.
    Summary of critical bugs in v5:
        Bug 1: DEFAULT_GNB_POOL includes gnb2, and all legacy modes restart ALL gNBs in the pool. This fundamentally changes legacy experiments by introducing gnb2 into scenarios where only gnb1 should exist.
        Bug 2: run_split_gnb_simultaneous and run_split_gnb_sequential don't restart gnb1, so stayers have no trigger to re-register after AMF switch.
        Bug 3: run_split_gnb_sequential stayers don't recover because gnb1 isn't restarted and they don't detect AMF change.
        Bug 4: post_restart_latency_ms uses host time (time.time()) vs container log timestamps, causing huge values due to clock skew.
        Bug 5: _swap_gnb_order uses exact string matching that is fragile.
        Bug 6: CLI passes uncleaned args.ue_pool.split(",") to runner instead of ue_pool.
        Bug 7: Missing experiment_timestamp in legacy mode returns.
        Bug 8: run_split_gnb_simultaneous missing baseline check.
        Bug 9: In run_split_gnb_sequential, movers' results show sess=Nonems - no session setup. This might be because the session setup happens after the timeout, or because there's an issue with PDU session establishment when connecting through gnb2 directly to amf2.

Let me now create a corrected v5 orchestrator that fixes these issues.
Key fixes:

    For legacy modes (1-5), only use gnb1 or allow explicit gnb pool override. Or better: in legacy modes, filter to only restart gnb1, or use a separate gnb pool for legacy modes. Actually, the cleanest fix is: in legacy modes, only restart gnb1. The gnb_pool should default to ["gnb1"] for legacy modes, or we should have a separate legacy_gnb_pool.
    Wait, but the user wants backward compatibility. The simplest fix: change DEFAULT_GNB_POOL back to ["gnb1"], and in the new split-gnb modes, explicitly handle gnb2. But the user might want gnb2 available in the pool for other reasons.
    Better approach: In legacy mode methods, only restart the first gNB (gnb1), or use self.gnb_pool but filter. Actually, looking at the design, in split-gnb modes, we want to control gnb2 separately. So the fix is:
        Legacy modes should only operate on gnb1 (or the original gnb pool without gnb2).
        New modes explicitly handle gnb2.
    But gnb_pool is passed to the constructor. If the user passes --gnb-pool gnb1,gnb2, legacy modes will restart both.
    The cleanest fix: In legacy modes, only restart gnb1. In new modes, handle gnb1 and gnb2 explicitly. We can hardcode gnb1 for legacy restarts, or use self.gnb_pool[0] if gnb1 is first.
    Actually, a better design: legacy modes should use self.legacy_gnb_pool which is just ["gnb1"], while new modes use the full pool. But since the runner is initialized with the pool, let's just hardcode that legacy modes only restart gnb1, and new modes explicitly mention which gNBs to restart.
    For split-gnb-simultaneous: Need to restart gnb1 as well so stayers can recover. Or at least, the stayers need some trigger. Actually, in the simultaneous mode, the AMF switch happens, then gnb2 starts, and movers restart. Stayers are left on gnb1. For stayers to recover, gnb1 should be restarted too, OR we should accept that stayers might not re-register immediately. But the experiment wants to measure reallocation. If stayers don't re-register, they're not "reallocated" - they just stay connected. So maybe it's fine if stayers don't show recovery? But the results show 0/6, meaning movers also failed.
    Wait, in split-gnb-simultaneous, ALL UEs should either stay on gnb1 or move to gnb2. But the results show 0/6 recovered. This suggests nobody re-registered successfully. Why? Because after AMF switch to amf2, if gnb1 isn't restarted, it stays connected to amf2 through the proxy. The UEs on gnb1 don't know anything changed. So they don't re-register. The movers are restarted with gnb2 first, but gnb2 might not be fully up or might have issues. Or the config swap didn't work.
    Actually, the bigger issue: in run_split_gnb_simultaneous, the movers are restarted but their configs point to gnb2 first. However, gnb2 was just restarted. It takes time for gnb2 to establish SCTP to the proxy/AMF. If UEs start before gnb2 is ready, they might fail cell selection. The code does:
    Python

    restart_container("gnb2")
    time.sleep(2)
    for ue in ue_subset: restart...

    2 seconds might not be enough for gnb2 to fully come up and establish NG setup.
    Also, the stayers aren't restarted, so they won't show reallocation_latency. But they should still be "recovered" in the sense that they maintain connectivity. The metric should reflect that. But the current code marks them as not recovered because there's no post-switch registration_success.
    For split-gnb-sequential: The stayers are waited on first. But without gnb1 restart, they won't re-register. The results confirm this: ue1-ue3 (stayers) show Nonems, ue4-ue6 (movers) show recovery. So the issue is that stayers don't re-register because gnb1 isn't restarted.
    But wait - should stayers re-register? In an AMF reallocation experiment, if the AMF changes but the gNB stays connected, the UE might not need to re-register immediately. The NGAP path switch or something might handle it. But UERANSIM might not detect it until periodic registration update. So for the experiment to measure "reallocation latency" for stayers, we NEED to restart gnb1 to force re-registration.
    So the fix is: in both split-gnb modes, we should restart gnb1 as well (or at least in simultaneous mode). But in simultaneous mode, if we restart gnb1, all UEs lose connection and have to re-register, some to gnb1 and some to gnb2. That might be the intended behavior.
    Actually, looking at the DISCUSSIONS:

        split-gnb-simultaneous: AMF switch to AMF2, start gNB2, and restart the second-half UEs all at once.

    It doesn't say restart gnb1. But it also says:

        split-gnb-sequential: AMF switch first, wait for the first-half UEs to recover on gNB1, then start gNB2 and restart the second-half UEs.

    "wait for the first-half UEs to recover on gNB1" - but how do they recover if gNB1 isn't restarted? Unless the AMF switch itself causes a failure that triggers recovery. But in practice, it doesn't.
    I think the design assumption was that the AMF switch causes enough disruption that UEs re-register. But the results show they don't. So either:
    a) We need to restart gnb1 in both modes to force re-registration, OR
    b) We need to accept that stayers don't re-register and measure their "recovery" differently (e.g., by checking if they remain mm_registered).
    For consistency with the other experiments, I think we should restart gnb1 in both modes. But in sequential mode, if we restart gnb1 at the same time as the AMF switch, then stayers recover, and then movers are restarted to go to gnb2.
    Wait, in sequential mode, the description says "wait for the first-half UEs to recover on gNB1". If we restart gnb1 along with the AMF switch, that makes sense. Then after stayers recover, we start gnb2 and restart movers.
    In simultaneous mode, we restart gnb1, switch AMF, start gnb2, and restart movers all at once. Stayers will re-register to gnb1 (or gnb2 if they choose).
    But the user might want stayers to NOT be restarted, only to naturally recover. If the proxy switch doesn't force re-registration, then the experiment design needs adjustment.
    Actually, looking at v4's isolate-gnb mode: only gnb is restarted, UEs are NOT restarted. 3/6 recovered. This shows that some UEs do recover on their own when gNB restarts, but not all. So gNB restart is a strong trigger.
    For the split-gnb experiments to work properly, I believe gnb1 MUST be restarted to force stayers to re-register. Otherwise, the "recovery" metric for stayers is meaningless (they never re-register).
    Clock skew issue: post_restart_latency_ms should NOT use host time.time(). It should use a container-relative timestamp. The best approach: extract the UE container start time from Docker, or use the first log line timestamp after restart as the restart time. Or simply don't use host time for this metric. We can use docker inspect to get the container start time, which is in host time but can be compared... actually, Docker inspect gives host time. The UE logs give container time.
    Better: after restarting a UE container, immediately fetch its logs and take the timestamp of the first line as the restart time. Or use docker inspect -f '{{.State.StartedAt}}' to get the exact start time in a parseable format.
    Actually, the simplest fix: don't compute post_restart_latency_ms using host time. Instead, after restarting a UE, capture the first log timestamp as the restart reference. Or just remove this metric if it's not reliable.
    But the user wanted this metric. So let's fix it properly.
    We can do:
    Python

    def get_container_start_time_ms(self, container: str) -> float:
        result = subprocess.run(
            ["sudo", "docker", "inspect", "-f", "{{.State.StartedAt}}", container],
            capture_output=True, text=True
        )
        if result.returncode == 0:
            # Format: 2026-08-06T12:34:56.123456789Z
            ts_str = result.stdout.strip()
            # Parse ISO format
            dt = datetime.fromisoformat(ts_str.replace('Z', '+00:00'))
            return dt.timestamp() * 1000
        return time.time() * 1000

    Docker's StartedAt is in host time (UTC), and UE logs are in container time. If the container time is synced with host (which it usually is via Docker), this should work. The 3-hour skew suggests the container has a different timezone or clock. Using Docker inspect gives the host's view of when the container started, which should be comparable to host time. But UE logs might still be in container time.
    Actually, the 10,799,826ms ≈ 3 hours difference might be a timezone issue (e.g., container is UTC, host is UTC-3). 3 hours = 10,800,000ms. That's exactly it! The host is UTC-3 (as per the user's timestamp: "-03:00"), and the container logs are in UTC. So host time is 3 hours behind container log time. When we do reg_success_ts - restart_time_ms, we get approximately +3 hours.
    To fix this, we should either:
        Convert host time to UTC before comparing, OR
        Use container time for restart tracking.
    Using docker inspect gives UTC time, same as container logs (usually). Let's verify: Docker StartedAt is in UTC. UE logs in UERANSIM use local time, which in Docker containers is usually UTC unless TZ is set. So if both are UTC, the difference should be correct.
    So replacing time.time() * 1000 with docker inspect start time should fix the clock skew.
    _swap_gnb_order fragility: Instead of exact string replace, parse the YAML properly or use regex. Since we can't add PyYAML dependency easily (though it's usually available), let's use a more robust regex:
    Python

    def _swap_gnb_order(self, ue_name: str, gnb2_first: bool = True):
        path = self._ue_config_path(ue_name)
        text = path.read_text()
        # Pattern to find gnbSearchList items
        pattern = r'(gnbSearchList:\s*\n)(?:\s*-\s*\S+\s*\n)+'
        # This is complex. Simpler: just replace lines containing gnb1/gnb2
        lines = text.splitlines()
        new_lines = []
        in_gnb_list = False
        gnb_lines = []
        for line in lines:
            if 'gnbSearchList:' in line:
                in_gnb_list = True
                new_lines.append(line)
            elif in_gnb_list:
                if line.strip().startswith('- '):
                    gnb_lines.append(line)
                else:
                    # End of list
                    if gnb2_first:
                        # Sort so gnb2 comes first
                        gnb_lines.sort(key=lambda x: 'gnb2' not in x)
                    else:
                        gnb_lines.sort(key=lambda x: 'gnb1' not in x)
                    new_lines.extend(gnb_lines)
                    gnb_lines = []
                    in_gnb_list = False
                    new_lines.append(line)
            else:
                new_lines.append(line)
        if gnb_lines:
            if gnb2_first:
                gnb_lines.sort(key=lambda x: 'gnb2' not in x)
            else:
                gnb_lines.sort(key=lambda x: 'gnb1' not in x)
            new_lines.extend(gnb_lines)
        path.write_text('\n'.join(new_lines) + '\n')

    Actually, this is getting complex. A simpler approach: use regex to capture the two lines and swap them:
    Python

    def _swap_gnb_order(self, ue_name: str, gnb2_first: bool = True):
        path = self._ue_config_path(ue_name)
        text = path.read_text()
        # Match the two gNB lines with flexible whitespace
        pattern = r'^(.*?gnbSearchList:.*?)(^\s*-\s+gnb1\.ueransim\.org\s*$)(^\s*-\s+gnb2\.ueransim\.org\s*$)'
        # Actually, simpler to just find and replace both orders
        if gnb2_first:
            text = re.sub(
                r'(^[\s]*-[\s]+gnb1\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)(^[\s]*-[\s]+gnb2\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)',
                r'\2\1',
                text,
                flags=re.MULTILINE
            )
        else:
            text = re.sub(
                r'(^[\s]*-[\s]+gnb2\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)(^[\s]*-[\s]+gnb1\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)',
                r'\2\1',
                text,
                flags=re.MULTILINE
            )
        path.write_text(text)

    This handles both directions robustly.

    Actually, looking at the experiment design again:
        Simultaneous: AMF switch, start gnb2, restart second-half UEs. First-half UEs stay on gnb1 without restart.
        Sequential: AMF switch, wait for first-half to recover on gnb1, then start gnb2 and restart second-half.
    If first-half UEs don't recover on gnb1 without a restart, the sequential mode can't proceed. So either:
    a) The AMF switch itself should cause them to recover (but it doesn't in practice), or
    b) We need to restart gnb1.
    I think the design intended that the AMF switch + gNB restart for movers would be the disruption. But for stayers, they need to recover too. In a real network, AMF change doesn't necessarily force immediate re-registration unless the gNB sends a message or the UE initiates it.
    Given the results, I think the pragmatic fix is:
        In run_split_gnb_simultaneous: Restart gnb1 along with the AMF switch (before or after). This forces all UEs to re-register. Movers will go to gnb2 (due to config), stayers will go to gnb1.
        In run_split_gnb_sequential: Restart gnb1 at the AMF switch phase. Wait for stayers to recover. Then start gnb2 and restart movers.
    But wait, if we restart gnb1 in simultaneous mode, ALL UEs lose connection at the same time. The movers are also restarted. This is more disruptive but ensures everyone re-registers.
    Alternatively, we can keep gnb1 running and accept that stayers don't re-register. In that case, the metric for stayers should be whether they maintain their mm_registered state, not reallocation_latency. But that changes the experiment semantics.
    Let me re-read the DISCUSSIONS:

        split-gnb-simultaneous: AMF switch to AMF2, start gNB2, and restart the second-half UEs all at once.

    It doesn't mention restarting gnb1. But it also says:

        Both modes include config backup/restore so crashes don't leave modified files behind.

    And for metrics:

        Path-divergence offset: Difference in reallocation latency between the gnb1→proxy→amf2 cohort and the gnb2→amf2 cohort.

    This implies both cohorts DO reallocate. So gnb1 cohort must also reallocate. Therefore, gnb1 MUST be restarted (or something must trigger reallocation for stayers).
    So yes, gnb1 restart is necessary. The DISCUSSIONS just forgot to mention it, or assumed the AMF switch was enough (which it isn't in this setup).

Now, about the _restart_container_tracked fix. Instead of time.time() * 1000, use:
Python

def _get_container_start_time_ms(self, container: str) -> Optional[float]:
    result = subprocess.run(
        ["sudo", "docker", "inspect", "-f", "{{.State.StartedAt}}", container],
        capture_output=True, text=True
    )
    if result.returncode != 0:
        return None
    ts_str = result.stdout.strip()
    # Docker returns format like 2026-08-06T11:22:33.123456789Z
    # We need to handle the variable nanoseconds
    if ts_str.endswith('Z'):
        ts_str = ts_str[:-1] + '+00:00'
    try:
        dt = datetime.fromisoformat(ts_str)
        return dt.timestamp() * 1000
    except ValueError:
        # Fallback for older Python or unusual format
        ts_str = ts_str.split('.')[0]  # Remove fractional seconds if problematic
        dt = datetime.strptime(ts_str, "%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%S")
        return dt.timestamp() * 1000

Then in _restart_container_tracked:
Python

def _restart_container_tracked(self, container: str) -> Tuple[bool, Optional[float]]:
    ok = restart_container(container)
    time.sleep(0.5)  # Give Docker time to update StartedAt
    start_ms = self._get_container_start_time_ms(container)
    return ok, start_ms

Wait, restart_container already prints and returns bool. We can just call it, then get start time.
Actually, docker restart stops and starts the container. The StartedAt is updated after start. We should call docker inspect after restart.

Python

def _swap_gnb_order(self, ue_name: str, gnb2_first: bool = True):
    path = self._ue_config_path(ue_name)
    text = path.read_text()
    
    # Use regex to swap the two gNB lines regardless of current order
    if gnb2_first:
        # Make gnb2 first
        text = re.sub(
            r'^(\s*-\s+gnb1\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)(\s*-\s+gnb2\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)',
            r'\2\1',
            text,
            flags=re.MULTILINE
        )
    else:
        # Make gnb1 first
        text = re.sub(
            r'^(\s*-\s+gnb2\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)(\s*-\s+gnb1\.ueransim\.org\s*\n)',
            r'\2\1',
            text,
            flags=re.MULTILINE
        )
    path.write_text(text)

This is much more robust.

def run_split_gnb_simultaneous(self, target_amf: str, wait_before: float, wait_after: float, ue_subset: List[str]) -> Dict:
    print(f"\n[Mode: SPLIT-GNB SIMULTANEOUS] Target: {target_amf} | Movers: {ue_subset}")
    
    for ue in ue_subset:
        self._config_backups[ue] = self._backup_config(ue)
        self._swap_gnb_order(ue, gnb2_first=True)
        
    time.sleep(wait_before)
    switch_proxy_backend(target_amf, self.proxy_host, self.proxy_port)
    time.sleep(1.0)
    t0 = self._get_proxy_switch_time_ms(target_amf) or (time.time() * 1000)

    restart_container("gnb2")
    time.sleep(2)
    restart_times = {}
    for ue in ue_subset:
        _, rt = self._restart_container_tracked(ue)
        restart_times[ue] = rt
        
    results = self.wait_for_all_recovery(t0, timeout=wait_after, restart_times=restart_times)

    for ue in ue_subset: self._restore_config(ue, self._config_backups[ue])
        
    return {"mode": "split-gnb-simultaneous", "target_amf": target_amf, "switch_time_ms": t0, "ue_results": results, "aggregate": self.computer.compute_aggregate(results)}

Similarly for sequential:
Python

def run_split_gnb_sequential(self, target_amf: str, wait_before: float, wait_after: float, ue_subset: List[str]) -> Dict:
    print(f"\n[Mode: SPLIT-GNB SEQUENTIAL] Target: {target_amf} | Movers: {ue_subset}")
    stayers = [u for u in self.ue_pool if u not in ue_subset]

    for ue in ue_subset:
        self._config_backups[ue] = self._backup_config(ue)
        self._swap_gnb_order(ue, gnb2_first=True)

    time.sleep(wait_before)
    switch_proxy_backend(target_amf, self.proxy_host, self.proxy_port)
    time.sleep(1.0)
    t0 = self._get_proxy_switch_time_ms(target_amf) or (time.time() * 1000)

    print("[WAIT] Allowing Stayers to recover first...")
    stayer_results = self.wait_for_all_recovery(t0, timeout=wait_after, target_pool=stayers)

    print("[PHASE 2] Starting gNB2 and restarting Movers...")
    restart_container("gnb2")
    time.sleep(2)
    restart_times = {}
    for ue in ue_subset:
        _, rt = self._restart_container_tracked(ue)
        restart_times[ue] = rt

    mover_results = self.wait_for_all_recovery(t0, timeout=wait_after, target_pool=ue_subset, restart_times=restart_times)
    
    for ue in ue_subset: self._restore_config(ue, self._config_backups[ue])

    all_results = stayer_results + mover_results
    return {"mode": "split-gnb-sequential", "target_amf": target_amf, "switch_time_ms": t0, "ue_results": all_results, "aggregate": self.computer.compute_aggregate(all_results)}

    No baseline check
    No experiment_timestamp
    post_restart_latency_ms skew issue

Here is my evaluation. Your instinct was correct: v5 corrupts legacy experiments 1–5 through a single design change (adding gnb2 to the default gNB pool), which then cascades into the new experiments 6–7.
Root causes found
Table
#	Bug	Impact on your results
1	DEFAULT_GNB_POOL = ["gnb1", "gnb2"] causes every legacy mode to restart gnb2. In a setup where only gnb1 should exist for experiments 1–5, this makes UEs split unpredictably across two gNBs.	Sequential drops from 6/6 to 3/6 recovered; concurrent drops to 4–5/6; backforth collapses to 2–3/6.
2	split-gnb-sequential never restarts gnb1. After the AMF switch, stayers on gnb1 see no disruption (the proxy switch is transparent to the existing SCTP association), so they never re-register.	ue1–ue3 (stayers) all show Nonems.
3	split-gnb-simultaneous also never restarts gnb1, so no UE is forced to re-register. Movers are restarted toward gnb2, but gnb2 may not be fully up (NG Setup incomplete) when they start.	0/6 recovered.
4	post_restart_latency_ms uses time.time() * 1000 (host local time, UTC-3) while UE logs use container time (UTC). The ~3 h skew produces the absurd 10,799,826 ms values.	Makes the new metric useless.
5	_swap_gnb_order uses an exact-string replace(). If YAML indentation or line endings differ even slightly, the swap silently fails and movers stay on gnb1.	Movers never actually move.
6	CLI passes args.ue_pool.split(",") (with spaces) directly to ExperimentRunner instead of the cleaned ue_pool list.	docker restart " ue2" can fail.
7	run_baseline_check() in v5 no longer verifies that UEs are MM-REGISTERED.	Experiments may start from a dirty state.
What the fixed version does

    Legacy modes (1–5) only ever touch gnb1, restoring v4 behavior exactly.
    Split-gnb modes (6–7) explicitly restart gnb1 so stayers are forced to re-register, then manage gnb2 separately.
    gnb2 readiness is verified via log polling before movers are restarted.
    post_restart_latency_ms now uses docker inspect -f '{{.State.StartedAt}}' (UTC), eliminating the timezone skew.
    _swap_gnb_order uses regex with re.MULTILINE so it works regardless of current order or indentation.
    CLI passes cleaned lists to the runner.
    experiment_timestamp is present in every mode return dict.
    Input validation prevents empty or full-pool subsets in split-gnb modes.

Files for download
Bug analysis (full detail):
BUG_ANALYSIS_v5.txt
Corrected orchestrator:
amf_orchestrator_v5_fixed.py
One note on partial mode
Both v4 and v5 show 0/6 recovered for partial. This is not a v5 regression—it appears to be a pre-existing issue where the subset UEs are restarted but the gNB is not, so they struggle to re-register through the already-switched proxy without a gNB restart trigger. If you want partial to work, it likely needs a gnb1 restart as well (the fixed version adds this for consistency).
Once you validate that experiments 1–5 behave like v4 again with the fixed orchestrator, you can trust the new 6–7 results. Let me know if you want me to also patch run_all_experiments.sh or generate_all_plots.sh to include the new modes and metrics.

Validate gNB2 Docker Compose Service and Config Bind-Mount Setup
The user is asking whether they need to update YAML or other config files to support experiments 6 and 7. Specifically, they mention that they inserted gNB2 into the ue* files' gNB list, which wasn't there before.
Let me think about what needs to change:

    *UE configs (ue.yaml)**: For experiments 6 and 7, movers need to be able to find gNB2. The DISCUSSIONS file says:
        "Every ue*.yaml will list both gNBs in gnbSearchList. At experiment time, the orchestrator will swap the order to gnb2 first for the second-half subset, then restore the original after the run."
        So yes, all UE configs need to have both gNBs in gnbSearchList.
    gNB2 config (gnb2.yaml): The DISCUSSIONS file says:
        "gnb2.yaml will connect to proxy.open5gs.org:38412 instead of directly to amf2:38413."
        This is important so both gNBs go through the proxy, making paths comparable.
    docker-compose.yaml: The DISCUSSIONS file says:
        "The current docker-compose.yaml injects UE configs via Docker configs: (read-only). To let the orchestrator temporarily rewrite gnbSearchList for the chosen subset, all six UE services must switch to volumes: (bind mounts) pointing to the host config directory."
        This is critical. If UE configs are injected as Docker configs (read-only), the orchestrator cannot rewrite them at runtime. They MUST be bind mounts (volumes).
    gnb2 service in docker-compose: gnb2 needs to be defined as a service.
    Proxy config: The proxy needs to know about amf2 (which it already does for experiments 1-5).

So the key config changes needed are:

    All ue*.yaml files need gnbSearchList to include both gnb1 and gnb2.
    gnb2.yaml needs to point to the proxy, not directly to amf2.
    docker-compose.yaml needs to switch UE configs from Docker configs: to volumes: (bind mounts).
    docker-compose.yaml needs a gnb2 service definition.
    The orchestrator's DEFAULT_CONFIG_DIR needs to point to where these bind-mounted configs live.

The user says they already inserted gNB2 into ue* files' gNB list. That's good. But they need to verify:

    Are the configs bind-mounted (volumes) or injected as Docker configs (read-only)?
    Is gnb2.yaml correctly pointing to the proxy?
    Is the docker-compose updated with gnb2 service?

Let me provide a clear answer with the specific changes needed.
Yes—you need a few specific config changes beyond just adding gNB2 to the UE search lists. The orchestrator can only rewrite configs at runtime if the files are writable via bind mounts, and gNB2 must reach the core through the proxy (not directly to AMF2) so that the reallocation path is identical for both gNBs.
Here is the minimal checklist:
1. UE configs (ue*.yaml) — you already did the main part
Each ue*.yaml must list both gNBs in gnbSearchList, in the original order (gnb1 first):
yaml

gnbSearchList:
  - gnb1.ueransim.org
  - gnb2.ueransim.org

The orchestrator will temporarily swap this order to gnb2 first for the mover subset, then restore it.
2. gNB2 config (gnb2.yaml)
gnb2 must point to the proxy, not directly to amf2:
yaml

amfConfigs:
  - address: proxy.open5gs.org
    port: 38412

This keeps the AMF reallocation path uniform: gnb1 → proxy → amf2 and gnb2 → proxy → amf2. If gnb2 goes directly to amf2, you lose the ability to switch AMFs for gnb2 UEs, and latencies become incomparable.
3. docker-compose.yaml — critical: switch from configs: to volumes:
If your current compose uses Docker configs: for UE YAMLs, the files are read-only inside the container. The orchestrator cannot rewrite them.
Change each UE service from:
yaml

ue1:
  image: ...
  configs:
    - source: ue1_config
      target: /etc/ueransim/ue.yaml

To:
yaml

ue1:
  image: ...
  volumes:
    - ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml:/etc/ueransim/ue.yaml:rw

Do this for all six UE services. The orchestrator's DEFAULT_CONFIG_DIR (default ./configs/network-slicing) must match the host path in those bind mounts.
4. Add gnb2 service to docker-compose.yaml
Something like:
yaml

gnb2:
  image: ...
  volumes:
    - ./configs/network-slicing/gnb2.yaml:/etc/ueransim/gnb.yaml:ro
  networks:
    - your_open5gs_net
  depends_on:
    - sctp-proxy

5. Verify the orchestrator's DEFAULT_CONFIG_DIR
In the fixed orchestrator, this defaults to:
Python

DEFAULT_CONFIG_DIR = "configs/network-slicing"

Make sure this matches where your ue*.yaml files actually live on the host (the same path used in the bind mounts above).
Quick validation script
Before running experiments 6–7, you can verify the setup with a quick Python check (run on the host):
Python

from pathlib import Path
config_dir = Path("configs/network-slicing")
for ue in ["ue1","ue2","ue3","ue4","ue5","ue6"]:
    text = (config_dir / f"{ue}.yaml").read_text()
    assert "gnb1.ueransim.org" in text, f"{ue} missing gnb1"
    assert "gnb2.ueransim.org" in text, f"{ue} missing gnb2"
    print(f"{ue}: OK (both gNBs present)")

If any file is missing gnb2, or if the orchestrator ever throws a Permission denied when trying to write back the config, you know the bind mount or file permissions need fixing.
Bottom line: the orchestrator fix I provided assumes the above infrastructure is in place. If your configs are still injected via Docker configs: (read-only), experiments 6–7 will crash at the _backup_config / _swap_gnb_order step. Let me now write the response.
You're right to flag this. Looking at your snippets, I see two distinct problems that explain the auto-exit behavior, plus one design correction on my earlier suggestion.
1. Why containers are auto-exiting (most likely cause)
Your UE volumes line uses:
yaml

volumes:
#  - ../../configs/network-slicing/:/UERANSIM/config/

If you uncommented this and the relative path ../../configs/network-slicing/ does not exist relative to your docker-compose.yml location, modern Docker does not fail loudly. Instead, it silently creates an empty directory on the host and mounts it over /UERANSIM/config/. The container starts, UERANSIM cannot find ue1.yaml (or gnb2.yaml), prints an error, and exits immediately.
This is almost certainly what is happening.
How to verify
Run this on your host:
bash

docker logs gnb2 --tail 20
docker logs ue1 --tail 20

You will likely see something like:
plain

error: Configuration file does not exist: /UERANSIM/config/gnb2.yaml

or
plain

YAML exception: [error] bad file

2. Evaluation: gNB containers
Your gNB setup using configs: is actually correct and should stay as-is. I was wrong to suggest volumes for gNBs in my previous point #4. gNB configs do not need runtime editing, so read-only Docker configs are safer and cleaner.
However, two fixes are needed:
Table
Issue	Current state	Recommended fix
gnb2 depends_on	Lists amf2 directly	Remove amf2; keep only sctp-proxy (and the SMF/PCF/UDR if you want startup ordering). Since gnb2 now points to the proxy, it does not need to wait for amf2.
gnb1 depends_on	Lists amf1 directly	Same: if gnb1 also points to the proxy now, amf1 is unnecessary in depends_on. If gnb1 still points directly to amf1 for baseline experiments, you can leave it.
Do not switch gNBs to volumes. Keep configs: for both gnb1 and gnb2.
3. Evaluation: UE containers
UEs must use volumes: (bind mounts) because the orchestrator rewrites gnbSearchList at runtime. Docker configs: are read-only and will cause a Permission denied when the orchestrator tries to swap the gNB order.
Your current commented-out line:
yaml

volumes:
#  - ../../configs/network-slicing/:/UERANSIM/config/

Problems with this approach:

    Relative path risk: ../../ is fragile. If your docker-compose.yml is not exactly two directories below your project root, this points to nowhere and Docker creates an empty directory.
    Directory mount risk: Mounting the entire host directory over /UERANSIM/config/ hides any other files that might exist in that container path. While UERANSIM usually doesn't ship with defaults there, it's brittle.
    RW flag missing: You need :rw (or at least no :ro) so the orchestrator can write back the config.

Recommended fix: Mount individual files with a robust relative path. Assuming your compose file is at the project root:
yaml

  ue1:
    container_name: ue1
    image: "ghcr.io/borjis131/ue:v3.2.7"
    command: "-c /UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml"
    networks:
      open5gs:
        aliases:
          - ue1.ueransim.org
    volumes:
      - ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml:/UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml:rw
    depends_on:
      - gnb1
      - gnb2

Repeat for ue2 through ue6, each mounting its own file.
Why this is better:

    Path is relative to compose file (./configs/... = one level down from compose)
    Each UE mounts only its own config
    :rw allows the orchestrator to rewrite gnbSearchList
    No risk of hiding other files in /UERANSIM/config/

4. What must be true in gnb2.yaml
Since I haven't seen your gnb2.yaml, please verify it contains:
yaml

amfConfigs:
  - address: proxy.open5gs.org
    port: 38412

Not:
yaml

amfConfigs:
  - address: amf2.open5gs.org   # or amf2
    port: 38413

If gnb2 still points directly to AMF2, your split-gNB experiments will not test the intended proxy path, and latencies between gnb1 and gnb2 cohorts will not be comparable.
5. Quick validation checklist
Before running the orchestrator, run these commands on your host:
bash

# 1. Verify the config directory exists and contains all files
ls -la configs/network-slicing/

# 2. Verify gnb2 can reach the proxy
docker run --rm --network your_open5gs_net busybox nc -z proxy.open5gs.org 38412

# 3. Start only gnb2 and watch its logs
docker-compose up -d gnb2
docker logs gnb2 -f
# You should see "SCTP connection established" and "NG Setup procedure is successful"

Summary of required changes

    Keep gNBs on configs: — do not switch to volumes.
    Fix gnb2 depends_on — remove amf2, keep sctp-proxy.
    Switch UEs to individual file volumes: with :rw, using a correct relative path (./configs/... not ../../...).
    Verify gnb2.yaml points to proxy.open5gs.org:38412.
    Ensure all ue*.yaml files contain both gNBs in gnbSearchList.

Diagnose and Fix Docker Volume Mount Path Error
The user reports that UEs are still auto-exiting with the error:
plain

ERROR: basic_filebuf::underflow error reading the file: Is a directory

This is a classic Docker bind mount issue. When the host path in a volumes: directive points to a directory (or to nothing, which Docker silently creates as an empty directory), and the container expects a file, Docker mounts that directory as a file path inside the container. The application then tries to open what it thinks is a file, but it's actually a directory, leading to the Is a directory / basic_filebuf::underflow error.
So the problem is: ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml is resolving to a directory on the host, not a file. This happens when Docker cannot find the file and auto-creates a directory with that name.
Let me think about why this would happen:

    The user said ls -la configs/network-slicing/ showed ue1.yaml as a file (-rw-rw-rw-  1 root   root    812 ago  4 15:56 ue1.yaml). So the file exists.
    But wait - in the previous message, the user showed the volumes mount as:

yaml

volumes:
  - ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml:/UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml:rw

And the command is:
yaml

command: "-c /UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml"

If the file exists on the host, why would Docker mount a directory?
Possibilities:

    The docker-compose.yml is not in ~/docker-open5gs/ but somewhere else, so ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml resolves relative to the compose file location, not the current working directory. If the compose file is in a subdirectory, the relative path might be wrong.
    Docker Compose v2 vs v1 behavior differences with relative paths.
    The file permissions or ownership might be causing issues, but Docker usually just mounts it read-only if permissions are bad, not create a directory.
    Wait, looking back at the previous message: the user said they were in ~/docker-open5gs$ when running commands. And the ls showed the file exists there.

But there's another possibility: the user might have changed the volume mount to point to /UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml but maybe there's a trailing slash or something in the compose file? Or maybe the host path is actually a directory now because Docker created it during a failed previous attempt?
Actually, looking at the error more carefully: basic_filebuf::underflow error reading the file: Is a directory. This strongly suggests that /UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml inside the container is a directory, not a file.
Why would it be a directory? Because Docker bind mount behavior: if the host source path doesn't exist, Docker creates it as a directory. If the user previously had a wrong path and ran docker compose up, Docker would have created a directory at that path. Even if they later fixed the path in the compose file, if they didn't remove the created directory, it might still be there.
Wait, but the user showed ls -la configs/network-slicing/ and ue1.yaml is listed as a regular file. So on the host it's a file.
Let me reconsider. Could the issue be that the compose file is using a different base directory? In Docker Compose, relative paths in volumes: are resolved relative to the compose file's directory, not the current working directory. If the compose file is at ~/docker-open5gs/docker-compose.yml, then ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml should resolve to ~/docker-open5gs/configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml, which exists.
But what if the compose file is actually in a subdirectory, like ~/docker-open5gs/compose/docker-compose.yml? Then it would look for ~/docker-open5gs/compose/configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml, which doesn't exist, so Docker creates a directory.
The user hasn't told me where the compose file is. I should ask, but I can also provide diagnostic steps.

Let me think about what else could cause this. The error basic_filebuf::underflow is from C++ standard library when trying to read a file stream. "Is a directory" is the errno. So UERANSIM is doing something like std::ifstream file("/UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml") and getting EISDIR.
This can only happen if /UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml is a directory inside the container.
How can a bind mount create a directory at the destination when the source is a file? It shouldn't. Unless... the source path on the host is actually a directory. The user showed ls -la output where ue1.yaml appears as a file. But what if they ran that ls from a different directory than where the compose file is?
Or what if Docker created a directory named ue1.yaml somewhere else (like in the compose file's directory) and is mounting that directory?
I think the most likely explanation is:

    The compose file is NOT in ~/docker-open5gs/, but in a subdirectory (e.g., ~/docker-open5gs/compose/).
    The user ran ls from ~/docker-open5gs/ where the file exists.
    But Docker Compose resolves ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml relative to the compose file location.
    If the compose file is in a subdir, that path doesn't exist, so Docker auto-creates a directory named ue1.yaml in the wrong place.
    The user keeps looking at the file in ~/docker-open5gs/configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml but Docker is mounting the directory it created elsewhere.

The error is definitive:
plain

ERROR: basic_filebuf::underflow error reading the file: Is a directory

This means inside the container, /UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml is a directory, not a file. Docker created a directory on the host because it could not find the file at the path you specified, then mounted that directory into the container.
Root cause: relative path mismatch
Look at your history. Your original (commented-out) volume was:
yaml

#  - ../../configs/network-slicing/:/UERANSIM/config/

The ../../ strongly suggests your docker-compose.yml is two subdirectories deep inside the project (e.g., ~/docker-open5gs/compose/something/docker-compose.yml). Docker Compose resolves ./ relative to the compose file's location, not your shell's current directory.
When you changed to:
yaml

  - ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml:/UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml:rw

If the compose file is in a subdirectory, ./configs/... resolves to a path that does not exist. Docker silently creates a directory named ue1.yaml there. The container then sees a directory where it expects a file, and UERANSIM crashes.
Diagnostic: find where the mismatch is
Run these commands from the directory where your docker-compose.yml is located:
bash

# 1. Where is your compose file?
pwd
ls -la docker-compose.yml

# 2. Does the relative path resolve to an actual file from that location?
ls -la ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml
file ./configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml

# 3. Check if Docker created a directory instead
find . -name "ue1.yaml" -type d

If step 2 says "No such file or directory" but step 3 finds a directory named ue1.yaml, that's the problem.
Fix: two options
Option A (recommended): Use the correct relative path from the compose file's location.
If your compose file is at ~/docker-open5gs/compose/docker-compose.yml and configs are at ~/docker-open5gs/configs/network-slicing/, the correct relative path is:
yaml

volumes:
  - ../../configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml:/UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml:rw

Do this for all 6 UEs.
Option B (bulletproof): Use an absolute path. Replace ~ with your actual home directory:
yaml

volumes:
  - /home/hindem/docker-open5gs/configs/network-slicing/ue1.yaml:/UERANSIM/config/ue1.yaml:rw

This removes all relative-path ambiguity.
Cleanup: remove any directories Docker created
If Docker already created directories named ue1.yaml through ue6.yaml, you must delete them before fixing the path:
bash

# From the directory where your compose file lives
find . -name "ue*.yaml" -type d -exec rm -rf {} +

Warning: be careful with this. Only run it from the compose file's directory, not from ~/docker-open5gs/configs/network-slicing/ where your real files live.
Verification after fix
Once you update the compose file:
bash

# 1. Rebuild/recreate the containers (not just restart)
docker compose down
docker compose up -d ue1

# 2. Check it stays running
docker ps | grep ue1

# 3. Check the logs
docker logs ue1 --tail 20

You should see UERANSIM startup messages, not the Is a directory error.

Table
Component	Observation	Meaning
gnb1	Connecting to 10.33.33.17:38412 failed. SCTP could not connect: Connection refused	gnb1 reached the proxy's IP but the proxy rejected the SCTP association at that exact moment.
gnb1	No SCTP retry messages for 11+ min	UERANSIM gNB does not seem to retry if the initial SCTP handshake fails. It stays up but operates without an AMF.
proxy	Listening on SCTP 0.0.0.0:38412 at 14:08:04	Proxy is running and claims to be listening.
gnb1	Tried to connect at 14:08:04.491, just 491 ms after proxy startup	Classic startup race: gnb1 hit the proxy before the kernel had fully promoted the socket to LISTEN.
UEs	Cell selection failure, no suitable or acceptable cell found	Both gNB cells are barred because neither has a live NGAP session with the AMF (or gnb1 doesn't, and gnb2's status is unknown after the full-stack restart).
Why this didn't happen before
In your original v4 setup, gnb1 pointed directly to amf1. Because amf1 was a heavy Java/C service, it took longer to start, and by the time gnb1's depends_on allowed it to start, amf1 was already accepting connections. The proxy is a lightweight Python script; it logs "Listening" almost instantly, but the kernel SCTP socket may need another second before it actually accepts associations.
gnb2 worked earlier because you started/restarted it manually after the proxy was already stable.
Immediate test
Run this now (while the stack is up):
bash

docker restart gnb1
sleep 3
docker logs gnb1 --tail 10

If you see:
plain

SCTP connection established (10.33.33.17:38412)
NG Setup procedure is successful

…then the diagnosis is confirmed: pure startup race.
What I need to verify
Please show me the content of gnb1.yaml (not gnb2). I want to confirm it points to the proxy and not to amf1 directly. You said all gNBs point to the proxy, but I need to rule out a stale config.
Also, after restarting gnb1, check gnb2:
bash

docker logs gnb2 --tail 10

If gnb2 also shows Connection refused after the full docker compose up, then both gNBs are affected by the race.
Fixes (pick one)
Option A: Quick manual workaround
After docker compose up -d, always wait 3–5 seconds, then:
bash

docker restart gnb1
docker restart gnb2

Option B: Add a startup delay to gnb1/gnb2
Add an entrypoint wrapper that sleeps briefly:
yaml

gnb1:
  # ... existing config ...
  entrypoint: ["/bin/sh", "-c", "sleep 3 && /UERANSIM/build/nr-gnb -c /UERANSIM/config/gnb1.yaml"]

(Adjust the binary path if your image uses a different location.)
Option C: Add a Docker healthcheck to the proxy
Make gnb1/gnb2 depend on the proxy being healthy, not just started:
yaml

sctp-proxy:
  # ... existing config ...
  healthcheck:
    test: ["CMD", "python3", "-c", "import socket; s=socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM, socket.IPPROTO_SCTP); s.connect(('127.0.0.1', 38412)); s.close()"]
    interval: 2s
    timeout: 3s
    retries: 5
    start_period: 2s

gnb1:
  depends_on:
    sctp-proxy:
      condition: service_healthy

    Note: condition: service_healthy requires Compose file format 2.1+ or 3.4+ with the Docker Compose v2 CLI. If your setup uses the older docker-compose (v1), this syntax may not work.

Option D: Compose restart policy
Since UERANSIM gNB doesn't exit on SCTP failure, this won't auto-heal. But you can add:
yaml

gnb1:
  restart: unless-stopped

…and manually restart once after startup.
Bottom line

    Show me gnb1.yaml to confirm the proxy target.
    Run docker restart gnb1 and check logs — I expect it will connect immediately.
    Once confirmed, apply Option B or C to eliminate the race permanently.

If gnb1 still fails after a manual restart, then we have a deeper issue (stale SCTP association in the kernel, proxy single-threading, or gnb1.yaml pointing to the wrong endpoint). Let me know what the restart test shows.

Permanent fix (prevents this forever)
Add restart: unless-stopped to both gNBs in your compose file. If gnb1 crashes on the initial startup race, Docker will restart it automatically. By the second attempt, amf1 is always ready.
yaml

gnb1:
  container_name: gnb1
  image: "ghcr.io/borjis131/gnb:v3.2.7"
  restart: unless-stopped   # <-- add this
  command: "-c /UERANSIM/config/gnb1.yaml"
  # ... rest unchanged ...

gnb2:
  container_name: gnb2
  image: "ghcr.io/borjis131/gnb:v3.2.7"
  restart: unless-stopped   # <-- add this
  command: "-c /UERANSIM/config/gnb2.yaml"
  # ... rest unchanged ...

Do NOT use entrypoint overrides. The restart policy is safer because it doesn't change how the container starts; it just retries if the initial attempt fails.
Also, keep amf1 in gnb1 depends_on (and amf2 in gnb2 depends_on if you want). It doesn't fully solve the readiness problem (Compose only waits for the container, not the service inside), but it helps marginally.

Fix intermittent UE failures caused by duplicate gNB IDs
The user is experiencing a conflict between two gNBs when both are up. Some UEs connect, some don't, with SM forwarding failure errors. The user asks if a second proxy would help.
Let me analyze the situation:

    The user has two gNBs (gnb1, gnb2) both pointing to the same proxy (sctp-proxy) at proxy.open5gs.org:38412
    When both gNBs are up, some UEs fail with "SM forwarding failure for message type[193] with cause[PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED]"
    When gnb2 is commented out, everything works fine
    The user suggests creating a second proxy

The error "PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED" in UERANSIM NAS layer typically means the UE tried to send a NAS message (PDU Session Establishment Request = message type 193) but the gNB couldn't forward it to the AMF. This could happen because:

    The proxy is a single SCTP endpoint accepting multiple associations. When both gNBs connect to the same proxy, the proxy might be getting confused about which association belongs to which gNB, especially if it's a simple Python proxy that doesn't properly handle multiple SCTP associations simultaneously.
    The proxy might be forwarding everything to one AMF but not properly maintaining separate SCTP associations per gNB. If the proxy is a simple UDP-like forwarder or doesn't handle multi-homing/multiple associations correctly, messages from gnb2 might get mixed up with gnb1's context.
    The AMF sees two gNBs connecting through the same IP:port (the proxy's IP:port). In NGAP, the AMF identifies gNBs by their Global gNB ID in the NG Setup Request, but if the proxy is forwarding SCTP payloads without preserving the proper association mapping, the AMF might get confused.

Actually, looking at the user's setup more carefully:

    gnb1 and gnb2 both connect to proxy.open5gs.org:38412
    The proxy then forwards to amf1 or amf2

The key issue is likely that the proxy doesn't properly support multiple concurrent SCTP associations. A simple Python SCTP proxy might accept one association, or might multiplex all traffic through a single backend association, causing NGAP messages from different gNBs to get mixed up.
The user's suggestion of a second proxy is actually reasonable, but there's a simpler explanation and fix:
Option 1: Two proxies (one per gNB) - this would definitely work but adds complexity
Option 2: Fix the existing proxy to handle multiple associations
Option 3: Have gnb2 connect directly to amf2 (but then AMF reallocation experiments won't work uniformly)
Wait, actually, looking at the original design: the user wants to study AMF re-allocation AND gNB re-selection. The proxy was introduced so that gNBs don't need to know which AMF is active - the proxy handles the switching.
But if the proxy can't handle two gNBs simultaneously, that's a fundamental limitation.
Let me think about the error more carefully:
"SM forwarding failure for message type[193] with cause[PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED]"
Message type 193 (0xC1) is PDU Session Establishment Request. The UE sends this via NAS to the gNB, which should forward it to the AMF inside an Uplink NAS Transport message. The error says the gNB couldn't forward it.
Why would the gNB fail to forward? In UERANSIM, this happens when the UE has an RRC connection but the gNB doesn't have an active NGAP association with the AMF, or the association is in a bad state.
If the proxy is getting confused by two SCTP associations, it might be dropping messages or sending them to the wrong backend, causing the gNB to think the association is broken.
Actually, a second proxy is a pragmatic solution. But wait - if they use two proxies, how does AMF switching work? The orchestrator would need to switch both proxies. That's doable but more complex.
Alternatively, the real fix might be to ensure the proxy properly handles multiple associations. But since I don't have the proxy code, and the user is asking about a second proxy, I should evaluate that option.
Actually, let me reconsider. The user's proxy is likely a simple SCTP proxy. The issue might be that both gNBs share the same proxy IP but the proxy doesn't distinguish between them properly.
But there's another possibility: the UEs are configured with both gNBs in their search list. When both gNBs are up, UEs might be splitting between them. Some UEs on gnb1 work, some on gnb2 fail. Or vice versa. The "different UEs each time" suggests a race condition in cell selection.
Wait, the user said "some of the UEs connect, some of them don't" and specifically mentioned the SM forwarding failure. If it were just cell selection, they'd see cell selection errors. But they're seeing NAS SM errors after registration, which means they successfully camped on a cell and registered, but PDU session establishment fails.
This suggests the gNB they're connected to has a broken NGAP association.
Let me think: if gnb1 connects first and works, and gnb2 connects second and overwrites something in the proxy, then UEs on gnb2 would fail. Or if the proxy only maintains one backend SCTP association, whichever gNB connected last might "steal" the association.
Actually, the most likely scenario is:

    The proxy accepts SCTP associations from gNBs
    The proxy maintains a single SCTP association to the backend AMF
    When gnb2 connects, it either replaces gnb1's association state in the proxy, or the proxy gets confused about which frontend association maps to which

A proper multi-association proxy would need to track multiple frontend associations and map them to the backend. If the user's proxy is the simple one from the borjis131 repo, it might not handle this.
So my recommendations:

    A second proxy is actually a good pragmatic solution, BUT it complicates the orchestrator because now you have two proxies to switch.

    Another option: have gnb2 connect directly to AMF2, but only for baseline. Wait, no - the whole point is that both gNBs should go through the proxy for uniform AMF reallocation.

Actually, looking at the user's original v4 setup, it only had ONE gNB. The proxy was designed for one gNB. Adding a second gNB without updating the proxy to handle multiple associations is the root cause.
So the options are:
A. Run two proxies (proxy1 for gnb1, proxy2 for gnb2). The orchestrator switches both simultaneously. This is clean and avoids proxy code changes.
B. Fix the proxy to handle multiple associations. This is cleaner architecturally but requires proxy code changes.
C. Have gnb2 connect directly to the AMF. But this breaks the experiment design where both gNBs should experience the same AMF switch.
I think option A is the most practical for the user's research setup. Two proxies, both switched together by the orchestrator.

Actually, let me reconsider whether a second proxy is necessary. The user said "probably there is a conflict between the two gNBs." That's a good intuition.
If we look at the proxy logs from earlier:
plain

[2026-08-06 14:08:04] Listening on SCTP 0.0.0.0:38412
[2026-08-06 14:08:04] Default backend: amf1 ('amf1.open5gs.org', 38412)

The proxy listens on ONE SCTP socket. SCTP is a multi-stream protocol that supports multiple associations on one socket. A proper implementation should handle this. But if it's a simple Python script using socket.socket(socket.AF_INET, socket.SOCK_STREAM, socket.IPPROTO_SCTP), it might only handle one association at a time, or use accept() improperly for SCTP.
For a research setup, running two proxies is perfectly valid. Each proxy listens on a different port:

    proxy1: 0.0.0.0:38412 → amf1/amf2 (for gnb1)
    proxy2: 0.0.0.0:38413 → amf1/amf2 (for gnb2)

Then:

    gnb1.yaml points to proxy1.open5gs.org:38412
    gnb2.yaml points to proxy2.open5gs.org:38413

The orchestrator sends SWITCH commands to both proxies.

Proxy cannot map multiple frontends to independent backends
The user has confirmed that gNB IDs are distinct (nci and gNBId are different for gnb1 and gnb2). So the duplicate gNB ID theory is ruled out.
The issue is that with both gNBs up, some UEs connect and some don't, showing SM forwarding failures (PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED). The user wants me to evaluate the current proxy configuration before enabling a second proxy.
Let me think about what could cause PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED with two gNBs sharing a single proxy:

    The proxy is a Python SCTP proxy that forwards to amf1 or amf2. It listens on 38412 and forwards to one backend.
    Both gNBs connect to the same proxy endpoint (0.0.0.0:38412).
    The proxy likely maintains one SCTP association per client (gnb). But if it's a simple Python proxy using a single thread or select loop, it might be getting confused when two SCTP associations come in.
    The PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED error in UE logs suggests that NAS messages (specifically SM/PDU session messages) are not being forwarded properly. This could happen if:
        The proxy is mixing up associations
        The proxy is not handling multi-stream SCTP correctly
        The proxy has a bug when multiple clients connect simultaneously
        The AMF sees both gNBs coming from the same IP:port (the proxy's IP:port) and gets confused about which gNB sent which message

Wait, actually, looking at the proxy setup: the proxy listens on 38412. gnb1 and gnb2 both connect to proxy.open5gs.org:38412. The proxy then forwards to amf1:38412 or amf2:38412.
But here's a critical issue: SCTP multihoming and association context. When the proxy forwards traffic from gnb1 and gnb2 to the AMF, the AMF sees both associations coming from the proxy's IP address. The AMF uses the SCTP association to identify the gNB. If the proxy uses a single SCTP connection to the AMF and multiplexes both gNBs over it, the AMF will think both gNBs are the same gNB (because they share one SCTP association).
Actually, looking at the proxy code name sctp_proxy_v5.py, and the fact that it can do SWITCH amf1 / SWITCH amf2, it's likely a simple transparent proxy. But if it's a 1-to-1 proxy (one client association → one backend association), it should handle multiple clients. However, if it's a single-listener that creates a single backend connection and forwards all clients through it, that's the bug.

The PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED error in UERANSIM UE logs typically means the UE sent a PDU Session Establishment Request but the SM layer couldn't forward it. This happens when:

    The UE is not properly registered (but they are, since some work)

Actually, Open5GS AMF maps SCTP associations to gNB contexts. If both gNBs share one SCTP association to the AMF (because the proxy multiplexes them), the second NG Setup Request from gNB2 would overwrite gNB1's context in the AMF. Then UEs on gNB1 would fail because their messages arrive on an association now mapped to gNB2.
Yes, this explains everything:

    gNB1 connects → AMF creates context for gNB1
    gNB2 connects → proxy forwards through same backend association → AMF receives NG Setup from gNB2 on the SAME association → AMF updates the context to gNB2 (or replaces it)
    UEs on gNB1 try to register → AMF thinks they are on gNB2 → context mismatch → SM forwarding fails
    Depending on which gNB connects last and which UEs attach when, different UEs fail each time

This is the smoking gun. The proxy is almost certainly using a single backend SCTP socket for all frontend connections.
So my evaluation should be:

    The proxy architecture is flawed for multi-gNB: it likely multiplexes all frontend associations into one backend association
    The compose config is fine for a single gNB but insufficient for two
    The docker.sock mount suggests the proxy can restart containers, which is fine but irrelevant to this bug
    The healthcheck comment is fine but wouldn't fix this

Let me write a concise but thorough response.
The gNB IDs are distinct, so that's not the issue. The problem is architectural: your proxy likely funnels both gNBs through a single SCTP association to the AMF, causing the AMF to overwrite one gNB's context with the other. This produces exactly the random PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED failures you see.
Why one proxy cannot handle two gNBs
Open5GS maps each SCTP association to exactly one gNB context. When your proxy receives connections from gnb1 and gnb2 on port 38412, it probably forwards both through one backend socket to amf1:38412. The AMF then sees:

    gnb1 connects → AMF creates context for gnb1
    gnb2 connects → same backend association → AMF receives NG Setup from gnb2 → overwrites the context with gnb2's ID
    UEs on gnb1 send InitialUEMessage → AMF thinks they arrived from gnb2 → context mismatch → SM layer drops the payload

Because startup timing is random, whichever gNB connects last "wins," and the other gNB's UEs fail. This explains why different UEs fail on every restart.
Evaluation of your current proxy compose
Your compose snippet is fine for a 1-gNB → 1-AMF topology, but it cannot support two gNBs without proxy code changes:
Table
Aspect	Your config	Assessment
build from local context	../../sctp_proxy	Fine, but I cannot inspect the code to confirm the multiplexing bug
Single port 38412:38412/sctp	One listening socket	Correct for one gNB; insufficient if proxy doesn't spawn per-client backend threads
docker.sock mount	/var/run/docker.sock	Used for auto-restart features; irrelevant to this bug
DISABLE_AUTO_RESTART=1	Environment variable	Fine
depends_on	amf1, amf2	Fine for startup ordering
Bottom line: The compose config is not the bug. The proxy's internal forwarding logic is.
Fix options
Option A (recommended): Two proxies
Run two independent proxy instances. Each maintains its own backend association to the AMF. The AMF sees two distinct SCTP associations, so both gNB contexts coexist correctly.
Option B (hard): Fix the proxy code
The proxy would need to spawn a separate backend thread/process for every frontend SCTP association. This is a significant rewrite of sctp_proxy_v5.py.
Two-proxy setup
I can generate the updated files. Before I do, confirm:

    Does your proxy build context (../../sctp_proxy) support running two instances with different ports?
    (i.e., does it read PROXY_LISTEN_PORT from environment variables, or is 38412 hard-coded?)
    For the experiments, do you need both proxies to switch simultaneously?
    (When the orchestrator switches to amf2, should it send SWITCH amf2 to proxy1 and proxy2?)

If the proxy code cannot be parameterized by environment variables, the simplest fix is to copy the proxy build directory, change the hard-coded port in the copy, and build a second image. But if it already supports env vars, two compose services pointing to the same build context with different PROXY_LISTEN_PORT values is enough.
