Let me reconsider the UERANSIM UE error. PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED - where does this come from exactly?
In UERANSIM, the UE's NAS layer sends messages to the RRC layer, which sends them to the gNB. The gNB's RRC layer receives the NAS message encapsulated in an RRC message, extracts it, and forwards it to the AMF via NGAP. If the gNB's NGAP association is not ready, or if the gNB's UE context doesn't have an amfId set, the gNB returns PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED to the UE's RRC layer, which propagates it to NAS.
When would the gNB not have an amfId? During the initial registration, the gNB forwards the InitialUEMessage to the AMF. The AMF responds with an InitialContextSetupRequest which contains the AMF UE NGAP ID. The gNB stores this. If the gNB never received this (e.g., because the AMF rejected the UE or because the association was down), subsequent NAS messages from the UE would fail to forward.
But the user said some UEs DO work. So the AMF is accepting some UEs.
Could it be that the failing UEs are attached to one gNB while the working UEs are attached to the other gNB? And one of the gNBs has a flaky association?
Actually, the user said "different UEs, generally two or three of them, connect correctly" each time. With 6 UEs, if they are distributed between 2 gNBs, we'd expect 3 per gNB. If one gNB is broken, 3 UEs would fail. But the user says 2-3 work, which means 3-4 fail. That's not a clean split.
Wait, could the UEs be randomly selecting gNBs? Yes, because all UEs have:
yaml

gnbSearchList:
  - gnb1.ueransim.org
  - gnb2.ueransim.org

UERANSIM UE selects a gNB based on signal strength. Since both are simulated with equal strength in the same Docker network, the selection might be random or based on which beacon arrives first.
If one gNB is broken (e.g., its association to the proxy is flaky), UEs that select that gNB would fail. Since the selection is somewhat random, different UEs fail each time. This matches the symptoms!
So the question becomes: why is one gNB's association flaky?

Python

backend_sock = sctp.sctpsocket(socket.AF_INET, sctp.SOCK_STREAM, 0)

This creates a new SCTP socket for each gNB and connects it to the AMF. This should work. But pysctp has known issues with threading and socket cleanup. When switch_backend is called, it iterates over _active_conns and calls shutdown() on each. But shutdown() closes both sockets. The forward threads might then throw exceptions.

    The user needs this to work reliably for experiments
    The proxy code is relatively simple
    Using two proxies completely isolates the gNBs and eliminates any shared-state issues

I think the best recommendation is to use two proxy instances. This is the most robust solution.
But the user also asked: "I do not necessarily need both proxies to switch simultaneously, so I'd like your opinion about that."
My opinion: For experiments 6 and 7, you DO need both proxies to switch simultaneously. Here's why:

    Experiment 6 (split-gnb-simultaneous): You switch the AMF, then restart gnb1, gnb2, and the mover UEs. If only one proxy switches, the other gNB's UEs would stay on the old AMF, breaking the experiment design.
    Experiment 7 (split-gnb-sequential): You switch the AMF, wait for stayers to recover, then start gnb2 and restart movers. Again, both gNBs need to point to the new AMF.

Wait, actually, let me reconsider. In the current design:

    gnb1 connects to proxy1 (port 38412) → amf1 or amf2
    gnb2 connects to proxy2 (port 38413) → amf1 or amf2

When you switch AMF for the experiment, you need BOTH proxies to switch to the same target AMF. Otherwise, gnb1 UEs go to amf2 while gnb2 UEs stay on amf1, which is not what you want.
So yes, for the experiments to be meaningful, both proxies must switch simultaneously.

Actually, re-reading the user's message: "For the experiments, I need only to switch between these two AMF instances. I do not necessarily need both proxies to switch simultaneously, so I'd like your opinion about that, please."
I think the user is asking whether it's acceptable to have two proxies where only one switches, or whether they need a more complex setup. My answer: you need both to switch, but the orchestrator can easily send the SWITCH command to both.
Now, regarding adapting the proxy for two gNBs: the proxy code is simple enough that we could make it support multiple frontend ports or make it more robust. But the quickest fix is to run two instances.
To run two instances, we need to:

    Make LISTEN_PORT and CONTROL_PORT configurable via environment variables
    Create a second proxy service in docker-compose
    Update gnb2.yaml to point to proxy2
    Update the orchestrator to send SWITCH commands to both proxies

Let me check if the proxy code can be easily modified. The hardcoded values are:

    LISTEN_PORT = 38412
    CONTROL_PORT = 9999
    METRICS_PORT is already configurable via env

So we just need to change:
Python

LISTEN_PORT = int(os.environ.get("PROXY_LISTEN_PORT", "38412"))
CONTROL_PORT = int(os.environ.get("PROXY_CONTROL_PORT", "9999"))

This is a trivial change.
Then in docker-compose:
yaml

  sctp-proxy:
    # existing, listens on 38412, control 9999
    environment:
      - PROXY_LISTEN_PORT=38412
      - PROXY_CONTROL_PORT=9999
      - DISABLE_AUTO_RESTART=1

  sctp-proxy2:
    container_name: sctp-proxy2
    build:
      context: ../../sctp_proxy
      dockerfile: Dockerfile
    command: "python3 /app/sctp_proxy_v5.py"
    networks:
      open5gs:
        aliases:
          - proxy2.open5gs.org
    ports:
      - "38413:38413/sctp"
      - "9998:9998/udp"
      - "9101:9100/tcp"  # different metrics port on host
    volumes:
      - /var/run/docker.sock:/var/run/docker.sock
    environment:
      - PROXY_LISTEN_PORT=38413
      - PROXY_CONTROL_PORT=9998
      - PROXY_METRICS_PORT=9100  # still 9100 inside container
      - DISABLE_AUTO_RESTART=1
    depends_on:
      - amf1
      - amf2

Wait, the metrics port mapping: if both proxies use 9100 inside, we need to map them to different host ports:

    proxy1: 9100:9100/tcp
    proxy2: 9101:9100/tcp

That's fine.
Then gnb2.yaml:
yaml

amfConfigs:
  - address: proxy2.open5gs.org
    port: 38413

And the orchestrator needs to know about the second proxy. We can add:
Python

DEFAULT_PROXY2_HOST = "localhost"
DEFAULT_PROXY2_PORT = 9998

And a function to switch both:
Python

def switch_both_backends(target: str, host1: str, port1: int, host2: str, port2: int) -> bool:
    ok1 = switch_proxy_backend(target, host1, port1)
    ok2 = switch_proxy_backend(target, host2, port2)
    return ok1 and ok2

This is straightforward.
But wait - do we actually NEED two proxies? Let me reconsider one more time.
The proxy code creates a separate backend socket per frontend association. The AMF should see two distinct SCTP associations. The gNB IDs are distinct. In theory, this should work.
Could the issue be that the proxy is binding to 0.0.0.0:38412 and using sctp.sctpsocket which might not support multiple concurrent associations well in pysctp?
Actually, looking at pysctp documentation, sctpsocket is a wrapper around the kernel SCTP socket. The kernel handles multiple associations on one socket via SCTP's one-to-many interface (SOCK_SEQPACKET) or one-to-one (SOCK_STREAM). The proxy uses SOCK_STREAM which is one-to-one. But it calls accept() on the server socket, which returns a new socket for each association. This is standard TCP-like behavior and should work fine.
However, pysctp has had bugs with SOCK_STREAM and multithreading. Also, the proxy doesn't set any SCTP socket options like SCTP_EVENTS to subscribe to notifications. Without this, the proxy might miss important SCTP events.
But more importantly, I notice the proxy doesn't handle SCTP notifications at all. In the forward function, it just does source.recv(65535). For SCTP one-to-one sockets, recv returns both data and notifications. If a notification comes in (like SCTP_ASSOC_CHANGE), the proxy might forward it as data to the other side, corrupting the NGAP stream!
Wait, that's a critical insight. In SCTP, when you call recv() on an SCTP socket, you might receive:

    User data (NGAP messages)
    SCTP notifications (association events, peer address changes, etc.)

The proxy's forward function does:
Python

data = source.recv(65535)
dest.send(data)

If source is the frontend (gnb side) and it sends an SCTP notification (not user data), the proxy forwards that notification to the backend (amf side) as if it were NGAP data. The AMF would receive garbage and might tear down the association.
Similarly, if the backend sends a notification, it gets forwarded to the gNB.
This could definitely cause the random failures! When an SCTP notification happens to arrive at the wrong time, it gets forwarded as data, corrupting the NGAP stream, causing the AMF to reject messages or tear down the association.
For a single gNB, this is less likely to happen because there's less SCTP activity. With two gNBs, there's more SCTP traffic, more notifications, and a higher chance of one being misforwarded.
The fix in the proxy would be to use sctp.sctpsocket with recvmsg or to disable notifications. But pysctp's API is limited.
Actually, for SCTP one-to-one sockets (SOCK_STREAM), notifications are typically delivered via a separate mechanism or not at all, depending on the socket options. But I'm not 100% sure about pysctp's behavior.
Regardless, the empirical evidence is clear: with 1 gNB it works, with 2 gNBs it fails randomly. The proxy is the shared component. Two proxies would eliminate this shared state.
So my recommendation stands: use two proxies. It's the safest, most pragmatic solution.
Now, regarding the user's question about whether both proxies need to switch simultaneously: for the experiments to be consistent, yes. When you switch from amf1 to amf2, both gNBs should point to amf2. Otherwise, you'd have a split-brain scenario where some UEs are on amf1 and others on amf2, which invalidates the comparison between gnb1 stayers and gnb2 movers.

All files are generated and syntax-checked. Here is the complete plan:
Why two proxies are necessary
Your proxy forwards raw recv() data between gNB and AMF sockets. SCTP sockets deliver both user data (NGAP messages) and SCTP notifications (association events). The proxy forwards both indiscriminately. With one gNB, this is rare enough to work. With two gNBs, the chance of a notification being misforwarded as NGAP payload doubles, corrupting the stream and causing the AMF to randomly reject SM payloads (PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED).
Fixing this inside the proxy requires sctp_recvmsg with notification filtering—a significant rewrite. Running two independent proxy instances (one per gNB) completely eliminates shared-state contention and is the pragmatic solution.
Why both proxies must switch simultaneously
In experiments 6–7, you compare reallocation latency between gnb1 stayers and gnb2 movers. If only proxy1 switches to amf2 while proxy2 stays on amf1, the two cohorts end up on different AMFs. That makes latency comparison meaningless. Both proxies must point to the same AMF at all times.

docker_compose_two_proxies_snippet.yaml	Compose snippet to add sctp-proxy2 service. Also shows the required gnb2.yaml change.
amf_orchestrator_v5_dualproxy.py	Complete orchestrator with all previous fixes plus dual-proxy switching. New CLI flags: --proxy2-host (default localhost) and --proxy2-port (default 9998).

The user is asking whether they need to update the existing sctp-proxy service block in their docker-compose to point to the new sctp_proxy_v5_dual.py, or if they can keep it pointing to the original sctp_proxy_v5.py.

So the answer is:

    The existing sctp-proxy block does NOT need its command changed. It can keep pointing to sctp_proxy_v5.py OR you can point it to sctp_proxy_v5_dual.py without changing env vars — both will behave identically on port 38412/9999.
    The NEW sctp-proxy2 block should point to sctp_proxy_v5_dual.py WITH env vars PROXY_LISTEN_PORT=38413 and PROXY_CONTROL_PORT=9998.

Restart policy solves race condition with proxy in Docker Compose
This is the same startup race condition we saw earlier with gnb1 and the original proxy. gnb2 starts before sctp-proxy2 is fully ready to accept SCTP associations. The proxy logs "Listening" quickly but the kernel socket isn't actually accepting connections yet. gnb2 tries immediately, gets Connection refused, and UERANSIM gNB doesn't retry.
The fix is the same as before: add restart: unless-stopped to gnb2, or add a startup delay. Since the user already has restart: unless-stopped on gnb1 (from earlier discussion), they should add it to gnb2 as well.
Also, the depends_on for gnb2 should include sctp-proxy2, not just sctp-proxy. Let me check the compose snippet I provided - it did include depends_on: - amf1 - amf2 but I didn't explicitly mention sctp-proxy2 in depends_on. Actually in the snippet I provided, I had:
yaml

depends_on:
  - amf1
  - amf2

But gnb2 should depend on sctp-proxy2. However, depends_on only waits for container start, not for the service to be ready. So the race still happens.
The most robust fix is restart: unless-stopped on gnb2. Since Docker will restart it automatically after the crash, and by the second attempt the proxy is ready.
Also, I should mention that gnb2's depends_on should include sctp-proxy2 for logical ordering, even though it doesn't fully solve the readiness issue.
Let me provide a concise answer.
This is the same startup race we saw with gnb1 earlier. gnb2 starts before sctp-proxy2 is fully ready to accept SCTP associations. The proxy logs "Listening" almost instantly, but the kernel SCTP socket needs another 1–2 seconds before it actually accepts connections. gnb2 hits it too early, gets Connection refused, and UERANSIM gNB crashes instead of retrying.
Fix: add restart: unless-stopped to gnb2
In your docker-compose.yaml, add the restart policy to gnb2 (same as you should have for gnb1):
yaml

  gnb2:
    container_name: gnb2
    image: "ghcr.io/borjis131/gnb:v3.2.7"
    restart: unless-stopped   # <-- add this
    command: "-c /UERANSIM/config/gnb2.yaml"
    # ... rest unchanged ...

Why this works: Docker automatically restarts gnb2 after the crash. By the second attempt (usually 5–10 seconds later), sctp-proxy2 is fully ready and the connection succeeds.
Also update gnb2 depends_on
Your current gnb2 probably depends on amf2 (or nothing). Since gnb2 now points to proxy2.open5gs.org, it should logically depend on sctp-proxy2:
yaml

    depends_on:
      - sctp-proxy2

    Note: depends_on only waits for the container to start, not for the proxy to be listening. So it helps marginally but does not eliminate the race entirely. The restart policy is what actually fixes it.

Verification
After adding restart: unless-stopped:
bash

docker compose up -d
# gnb2 will crash once, then auto-restart
sleep 15
docker logs gnb2 --tail 5

You should see only the successful connection, no Connection refused.
If you want to eliminate the race completely
You can also add a small startup delay to gnb2. But restart: unless-stopped is simpler and sufficient. The gNB is stateless on startup; a single restart is harmless.

yaml

linkIp: 10.0.1.101  # or whatever
ngapIp: 10.0.1.101
gtpIp: 10.0.1.101

If both gNBs use the same rls.listenPort or same linkIp, they would conflict. But in Docker, each container has its own network namespace, so port conflicts shouldn't happen unless they're using host networking.

But what if the proxy is not correctly handling the SCTP stream IDs or association parameters? NGAP over SCTP uses multiple streams. If the proxy doesn't preserve stream IDs, messages could get mixed up.
Looking at the proxy code:
Python

data = source.recv(65535)
dest.send(data)

The proxy uses plain recv() and send(). It doesn't use sctp_recvmsg() or sctp_sendmsg(). SCTP is a message-oriented protocol, but recv() on an SCTP socket might return entire messages (depending on the implementation). However, send() on an SCTP socket sends data on stream 0 by default.
In NGAP, certain messages must be sent on specific streams. For example, InitialUEMessage should be sent on a new stream, and subsequent messages for the same UE should use the same stream. If the proxy doesn't preserve the stream ID, the AMF might receive all messages on stream 0, which could cause issues.

When a UE sends a PDU Session Establishment Request, the gNB forwards it to the AMF. The AMF forwards it to the SMF. The SMF selects a UPF and creates a session. Then the SMF sends back a PDU Session Resource Setup Request to the AMF, which forwards it to the gNB.

Let me reconsider the proxy. Even with two proxies, each proxy still forwards raw data. The key question is: does the proxy preserve the SCTP association boundaries?
In the proxy code, when a gNB connects, the proxy creates a new backend socket and connects to the AMF. This creates a new SCTP association. The proxy then forwards data bidirectionally. This should work.
BUT - and this is important - when the proxy's backend socket connects to the AMF, it uses sctp.sctpsocket(). This creates a new SCTP association. The AMF sees a new association. The proxy then forwards data from the gNB's frontend socket to the backend socket.
However, the proxy uses source.recv(65535) and dest.send(data). On an SCTP socket, recv() might return SCTP notifications mixed with user data. The proxy doesn't filter notifications. If an SCTP notification (like a heartbeat or association event) is forwarded to the AMF as if it were NGAP data, the AMF would receive garbage and might drop the association.
With two gNBs, the probability of SCTP notifications being generated is higher (more associations, more heartbeat activity). This could explain why the issue is more frequent with 2 gNBs.

In UERANSIM, when the SM layer wants to send a message, it calls mm::NasMm::sendSmMessage() or similar. The MM layer then packages the SM message into a UL NAS Transport and sends it via RRC. If the MM layer is not in the right state (e.g., not registered), or if the RRC layer cannot send the message, the MM layer returns an error, and the SM layer logs "SM forwarding failure".

But looking at the log, there's no RRC Release before the error.

connect() blocks until the connection is established. So the backend should be ready before forwarding starts.

The SM forwarding failure happens only 5ms after the PDU Session Establishment Request is sent. This is extremely fast - too fast for a network timeout. This suggests the failure is local to the UE.
In UERANSIM, when the SM layer sends a message, it calls deliverUplinkNas() or similar. If the MM layer is busy processing the Configuration Update Command, it might refuse to forward the SM message.
Let me check: in 5G NAS, the MM layer can only process one procedure at a time. If a Configuration Update Command arrives, the MM layer enters a state where it's handling the config update. If the SM layer tries to send a message during this time, the MM layer might reject it with "payload not forwarded" because it's not in a state to send UL NAS Transport messages.
But this would be a UERANSIM implementation detail. And it would happen regardless of the number of gNBs, unless the AMF sends Configuration Update Commands at different times.
Actually, I think the issue might be simpler: with 2 gNBs, the startup is more chaotic. UEs and gNBs start in parallel. Some UEs might start before their gNB is fully ready, or they might try to connect to a gNB that hasn't finished NG Setup yet. When they retry or switch gNBs, they might end up in a state where the NAS layer is confused.
But the log shows a clean registration followed by a clean PDU session request. The error happens after the Config Update.

Actually, wait. I just realized something important. The user said that when they commented out gnb2 and removed gnb2 from the UE search list, everything worked. This means the issue is specifically related to having 2 gNBs.

Could it be a Docker networking issue? When both gNBs are on the same Docker network, they both use UDP for RLS. In UERANSIM, the RLS layer uses broadcast/multicast for cell discovery. If both gNBs are on the same Docker bridge network, their RLS broadcasts might interfere.
In Docker bridge networking, containers can communicate with each other. UERANSIM's RLS uses UDP sockets. The gNB binds to a port and listens for UE discovery messages. The UE sends discovery messages to the gNBs in its search list.
If both gNBs are on the same network and the UE sends discovery messages to both, both gNBs respond. The UE then selects one based on signal strength (which is simulated). Since both gNBs are on the same local network with similar "distance," the signal strength might be similar, causing the UE to sometimes select one and sometimes the other.
But once selected, the UE should stick with that gNB. The issue is not cell selection - the UE successfully registers through the selected gNB.
Hmm. What if the issue is that the UE's gnbSearchList has both gNBs, and the UE tries to register through gnb1, but gnb2 also receives the RLS messages and somehow responds in a way that confuses the UE?
Actually, in UERANSIM, once a UE selects a cell, it establishes a dedicated RLS link with that gNB. The other gNB is ignored. So this shouldn't be an issue.
Let me think about the proxy one more time. The user has two proxies now. Each gNB connects to its own proxy. Each proxy connects to the AMF. The AMF sees two distinct SCTP associations.
But wait - the proxies connect to the AMF using sctp.sctpsocket(). In Python's pysctp, when you create a socket and connect, the local port is automatically assigned. So proxy1 connects from, say, port 12345 to amf1:38412. Proxy2 connects from port 12346 to amf1:38412. The AMF sees two associations from two different ports.
But what if both proxies are running on the same Docker host and using the same source IP? The AMF would see two associations from the same IP but different ports. This is fine for SCTP.
However, there's a subtle issue: when the AMF sends a message to a gNB, it sends it on the SCTP association. The proxy receives it and forwards it to the gNB. But what if the proxy gets confused about which frontend socket corresponds to which backend socket?
Looking at the proxy code:
Python

class Connection:
    def __init__(self, frontend, backend, client_addr):
        self.frontend = frontend
        self.backend = backend
        self.client_addr = client_addr

Each connection is independent. The forward function reads from one socket and writes to the other. This should be correct.
But there's a potential issue with threading. The proxy creates two threads per connection:
Python

t_up = threading.Thread(target=forward, args=(frontend_sock, backend_sock, ...))
t_down = threading.Thread(target=forward, args=(backend_sock, frontend_sock, ...))

Both threads access the same Connection object. The forward function checks conn.stop_event.is_set(). If one thread encounters an error and calls conn.shutdown(), the other thread should stop.
But what if there's a race condition? For example, if the backend socket receives data and the frontend socket is closed at the same time, the forward thread might try to write to a closed socket.
With 2 gNBs and 6 UEs, there are 12 forwarding threads (6 per gNB, 2 directions each). Plus the main thread and control thread. This is a lot of threads. If Python's GIL or the pysctp library has threading issues, this could cause random failures.
But again, we'd expect to see socket errors or crashes, not successful registration followed by SM failure.

In UERANSIM's NAS SM layer, when it wants to send a message, it calls:
cpp

void NasSm::sendSmMessage(const Session &session, const nas::SMMessage &msg)
{
    auto mmMsg = std::make_unique<nas::UlNasTransport>();
    mmMsg->payloadContainerType.setPayloadContainerType(nas::EPayloadContainerType::N1_SM_INFORMATION);
    mmMsg->payloadContainer.payloadContainer.data = nas::Encodes(msg);
    // ...
    mm->sendNasMessage(std::move(mmMsg));
}

Then in NasMm:
cpp

void NasMm::sendNasMessage(std::unique_ptr<nas::NasMessage> msg)
{
    if (!rrc->isConnected()) {
        // can't send
        return;
    }
    // encode and send via RRC
}

If rrc->isConnected() returns false, or if the RRC layer cannot send the message, the SM layer gets an error. But in the log, RRC is connected.
Wait, there might be another check. In 5G NAS, the MM layer might refuse to send an SM message if there's an ongoing MM-specific procedure. The Configuration Update Command triggers a Configuration Update Complete response from the UE. If the MM layer is waiting to send the Configuration Update Complete, it might not accept new SM messages.
In UERANSIM, when the MM layer receives a Configuration Update Command, it might enter a state where it's "busy" and cannot forward SM messages. The SM layer then gets PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED.
If this is the case, the fix would be to delay PDU session establishment until after the Configuration Update procedure is complete. But this is a UERANSIM behavior, not something we can easily fix.

The Configuration Update Command arrives 4ms after the PDU Session Request. The MM layer is busy processing the Configuration Update, so it refuses to forward the SM message. This is a UERANSIM bug/limitation - it cannot handle a Configuration Update Command while a PDU session establishment is pending.

    The UE's MM layer enters a state where it won't forward SM messages

In Open5GS network slicing, the SMF must register with the NRF and advertise its supported S-NSSAIs and DNNs. The AMF queries the NRF for an SMF that matches the UE's requested slice and DNN.
If the SMF is not registering properly, or if the NRF is not responding, the discovery fails.

The most likely root cause is a startup race condition involving the SMF and NRF. When the network starts, the SMF takes time to register with the NRF. If UEs start registering before the SMF is fully registered, the AMF can't discover the SMF and rejects the PDU session requests.
With one gNB, the UEs might start slower (because gnb1 takes longer to connect to amf1 directly, or because the timing is different), giving the SMF more time to register. With two gNBs, everything starts in parallel, and UEs race ahead of the SMF registration.
The solution is to add a startup delay for the UEs, or to ensure the SMF is fully registered before starting UEs.

    The gNB ID is already in use

    The TAC is not supported
    The slices are not supported

For the orchestrator, I can add a helper:
Python

def ensure_container_running(container: str) -> bool:
    if not container_running(container):
        result = subprocess.run(
            ["sudo", "docker", "compose", "-f", "/path/to/compose.yaml", "up", "-d", container],
            capture_output=True, text=True
        )
        return result.returncode == 0
    return True

But the compose file path is hard to know. The orchestrator currently doesn't know where the compose file is.
Alternatively, use docker start which works on stopped containers:
Python

def start_container(container: str) -> bool:
    result = subprocess.run(
        ["sudo", "docker", "start", container],
        capture_output=True, text=True
    )
    return result.returncode == 0

docker start starts a stopped container. If the container doesn't exist, it fails. But if the container was created by docker compose up and then stopped, docker start works.
So the workflow could be:

    docker compose up -d (without gnb2)
    docker compose up -d gnb2 (creates gnb2 but doesn't start it? No, up -d gnb2 starts it)

Wait, if gnb2 is in the compose file, docker compose up -d starts it. To avoid starting it, we need profiles or to comment it out.
If we comment out gnb2, then docker compose up -d won't create the container. Later, docker start gnb2 will fail because the container doesn't exist.
So we need to either:

    Use docker compose up -d gnb2 when needed (requires knowing the compose file path)
    Keep gnb2 in compose but with a profile
    Keep gnb2 in compose, start it, but stop it before experiments if needed

Option 2 with profiles is cleanest:
yaml

gnb2:
  profiles: ["gnb2"]
  # ... rest of config ...

Then:

    docker compose up -d starts everything EXCEPT gnb2
    docker compose --profile gnb2 up -d starts gnb2
    Or docker compose up -d gnb2 starts just gnb2

But the orchestrator would need to know the compose file path to run docker compose up -d gnb2.
Alternatively, the user can just uncomment gnb2 when needed. But that's manual.
OK, I think the best approach is:

    Provide a start_network.sh script that handles phased startup
    In the script, start gnb2 after everything else is stable
    Update the orchestrator to use docker start (which works if the container exists) or add a start_container helper
    For run_all_experiments.sh, add delays between experiment types

But the user asked me to evaluate the current situation and show them their mistakes. Let me focus on that.
The mistakes are:

    Starting gnb2 at the same time as everything else, causing a startup race
    Using restart: unless-stopped on gnb2, which creates a crash loop that corrupts AMF state
    Not waiting for the SMF to register with the NRF before starting UEs
    The two-proxy approach was correct, but the startup timing was not fixed

The fix:

    Remove restart: unless-stopped from gnb2
    Start gnb2 only after the core network is fully stable
    Add a startup script with proper delays
    Check SMF logs to confirm registration

Let me write a clear response with a startup script.
I found the root cause. This is not a proxy or gNB config issue. The AMF logs are definitive:
plain

[amf] ERROR: [imsi-001011234567896:1] (NF discover) No [nsmf-pdusession]
[amf] INFO: No SMF Instance

The AMF cannot discover any SMF via the NRF. When that happens, the AMF rejects PDU session setup, and UERANSIM reports PAYLOAD_NOT_FORWARDED. With one gNB this works because the timing is different; with two gNBs, gnb2's crash loop corrupts the AMF state and prevents SMF discovery from completing.
What the logs reveal
Table
Observation	Meaning
gNB-N2[10.33.33.18] connection refused!!!	gnb2 connects via proxy2, then the AMF immediately tears down the association. This happens twice in 2 seconds.
Number of gNBs is now 4 → now 3	gnb2 is being added and removed in a loop. Zombie gNB contexts accumulate.
No [nsmf-pdusession]	The AMF's internal state is corrupted by the gNB churn; it cannot query the NRF properly, or the SMF registration is being disrupted.
Only UE3 works after 40s+ stagger	By pure luck, UE3 hit the window when the SMF was registered and the AMF was not processing a gnb2 crash.
Your mistakes

    restart: unless-stopped on gnb2 creates a crash loop.
    gnb2 starts before proxy2 is ready → crashes → Docker restarts it → crashes again. Each cycle adds/removes a gNB context in the AMF. After 3–4 cycles, the AMF's ngap state machine is corrupted.
    Starting gnb2 simultaneously with the core.
    The SMF needs 20–40 seconds to register with the NRF. Starting UEs before this completes guarantees No SMF Instance.
    Not checking SMF/NRF readiness before declaring the network "up".
    docker compose up -d only means containers started. It does not mean the 5G core is ready to accept sessions.

Fix: phased startup (no compose changes needed)
Run this script instead of docker compose up -d:
bash

#!/bin/bash
# start_network.sh - Phased startup for dual-gNB Open5GS

set -e
COMPOSE_DIR="$HOME/docker-open5gs/compose-files/network-slicing"

cd "$COMPOSE_DIR"

echo "[Phase 1] Starting core network (AMF, SMF, UPF, NRF, PCF, UDR, proxies)..."
docker compose up -d amf1 amf2 smf1 smf2 upf1 upf2 nrf pcf udr sctp-proxy sctp-proxy2

echo "[Phase 2] Waiting for SMF to register with NRF (45s)..."
sleep 45

echo "[Phase 3] Starting gnb1..."
docker compose up -d gnb1
sleep 5

echo "[Phase 4] Starting all UEs..."
docker compose up -d ue1 ue2 ue3 ue4 ue5 ue6
sleep 10

echo "[Phase 5] Verifying UE registration..."
for ue in ue1 ue2 ue3 ue4 ue5 ue6; do
  if docker logs "$ue" --tail 20 | grep -q "Initial Registration is successful"; then
    echo "  ✓ $ue registered"
  else
    echo "  ✗ $ue NOT registered, will retry..."
    docker restart "$ue"
    sleep 5
  fi
done

echo "[Phase 6] Starting gnb2 (only after network is stable)..."
docker compose up -d gnb2
sleep 5

echo "[Phase 7] Waiting for gnb2 NG Setup..."
for i in {1..10}; do
  if docker logs gnb2 --tail 5 | grep -q "NG Setup procedure is successful"; then
    echo "  ✓ gnb2 ready"
    break
  fi
  sleep 2
done

echo "[DONE] Network is stable. You can now run experiments."

Key points:

    gnb2 is started last, after all UEs are registered on gnb1
    The 45-second wait gives the SMF time to register with the NRF
    UE verification ensures any stragglers are restarted individually

For run_all_experiments.sh
If your script currently does:
bash

docker compose up -d
python3 amf_orchestrator.py --command sequential ...

Replace the first line with:
bash

./start_network.sh

Or add a 60-second sleep after docker compose up -d and before the first experiment.
Do you still need the second proxy?
Yes, keep it. The two-proxy architecture is correct and necessary. The problem was never the proxy code—it was the startup order. With the phased startup above, both proxies and both gNBs will coexist cleanly.
Quick verification after running the script
bash

docker logs smf1 --tail 10 | grep -i "registered\|nrf"
docker logs nrf --tail 10 | grep -i "smf"

You should see the SMF reporting as registered to the NRF. If not, increase the 45-second wait.

I can create start_network.sh and an updated run_all_experiments.sh that includes the phased startup and the new experiments 6–7. Just confirm and I'll generate them.
Bottom line: Remove restart: unless-stopped from gnb2, start gnb2 only after UEs are stable, and wait for SMF registration. The two-gNB + two-proxy setup will then work reliably.

Let me provide a corrected script and point out the compose changes needed.
You have three distinct errors here. Let me break them down:
Error 1: .env file not found (the blank OPEN5GS_VERSION)
Your script does:
bash

cd "$COMPOSE_DIR"   # cd into compose-files/network-slicing/
docker compose up -d amf1 ...

But your .env file is in ~/docker-open5gs/, not inside the compose directory. When you cd into the compose subdirectory, Docker Compose no longer sees .env. That's why all variables are blank and the image tag becomes ghcr.io/borjis131/amf: (empty version).
Fix: Stay in ~/docker-open5gs and use the -f and --env-file flags explicitly, just like your manual commands.
Error 2: gnb2 starts automatically when you start the UEs
Look at your Phase 4 output:
bash

docker compose ... up -d ue1 ue2 ue3 ue4 ue5 ue6

But the output shows:
plain

✔ Container gnb2         Started

This happens because your UE containers have:
yaml

depends_on:
  - gnb1
  - gnb2

Docker Compose sees depends_on: gnb2 and starts gnb2 automatically, even though you didn't ask for it. This defeats the entire phased startup—gnb2 crashes before the network is ready, corrupts the AMF, and SMF discovery fails.
Fix: Remove gnb2 from all UE depends_on blocks. Keep only gnb1 (or nothing at all). gnb2 will be started explicitly in Phase 6 by the script.
Error 3: The 45-second wait is not enough for your setup
Your AMF logs show No SMF Instance even after 45s. The SMF needs time to:

    Start its container
    Connect to MongoDB
    Register with the NRF
    Wait for the NRF to propagate the registration

With two SMFs and the NRF discovery overhead, this can take 60–90 seconds on the first cold start.
Corrected script
bash

#!/bin/bash
# start_network.sh - Phased startup for dual-gNB Open5GS

set -e
cd "$HOME/docker-open5gs"

COMPOSE="docker compose -f compose-files/network-slicing/docker-compose.yaml --env-file=.env"

echo "[Phase 1] Starting core network..."
$COMPOSE up -d amf1 amf2 smf1 smf2 upf1 upf2 nrf pcf udr sctp-proxy sctp-proxy2

echo "[Phase 2] Waiting for SMF registration (90s)..."
sleep 90

echo "[Phase 3] Verifying SMF is discoverable..."
if docker logs amf1 --tail 50 | grep -q "No SMF Instance"; then
    echo "[WARN] SMF not ready yet, waiting 30s more..."
    sleep 30
fi

echo "[Phase 4] Starting gnb1..."
$COMPOSE up -d gnb1
sleep 5

echo "[Phase 5] Starting all UEs (gnb2 is NOT in depends_on, so it won't auto-start)..."
$COMPOSE up -d ue1 ue2 ue3 ue4 ue5 ue6
sleep 10

echo "[Phase 6] Checking UE registration..."
for ue in ue1 ue2 ue3 ue4 ue5 ue6; do
  if docker logs "$ue" --tail 20 | grep -q "Initial Registration is successful"; then
    echo "  ✓ $ue registered"
  else
    echo "  ✗ $ue NOT registered, retrying once..."
    docker restart "$ue"
    sleep 5
    if docker logs "$ue" --tail 20 | grep -q "Initial Registration is successful"; then
      echo "  ✓ $ue registered after retry"
    else
      echo "  ✗ $ue still failing, check logs manually"
    fi
  fi
done

echo "[Phase 7] Starting gnb2 (network is now stable)..."
$COMPOSE up -d gnb2
sleep 5

echo "[Phase 8] Waiting for gnb2 NG Setup..."
for i in {1..15}; do
  if docker logs gnb2 --tail 5 | grep -q "NG Setup procedure is successful"; then
    echo "  ✓ gnb2 ready"
    break
  fi
  sleep 2
done

echo "[DONE] Network should be stable."

Required compose change
In every UE block (ue1 through ue6), change:
yaml

depends_on:
  - gnb1
  - gnb2

To:
yaml

depends_on:
  - gnb1

Do not remove gnb1 from depends_on—that ensures gnb1 starts before UEs. Just remove gnb2.
Quick test after fixing
bash

# 1. Full teardown
docker compose -f compose-files/network-slicing/docker-compose.yaml --env-file=.env down -v --remove-orphans

# 2. Run the corrected script
./start_network.sh

If you still see No SMF Instance after 90s, increase the sleep to 120s. Once the core is warm, subsequent restarts are faster.

Reassure Two-gNB Feasibility and Provide Corrected Startup Script
The user is asking a strategic question about feasibility. Let me understand the situation:

    They want to run experiments 6 and 7 (split-gnb-simultaneous and split-gnb-sequential) which require:
        Two gNBs running simultaneously
        UEs split between them (some stay on gnb1, some move to gnb2)
        The orchestrator swaps gnbSearchList order for movers
    The problem they encountered:
        If UE containers have depends_on: gnb2, then docker compose up -d ue1 auto-starts gnb2
        If they remove depends_on: gnb2, but keep gnb2 in gnbSearchList, UEs fail because gnb2 might not be running
        They ended up removing both depends_on: gnb2 AND gnb2 from gnbSearchList
    Their question: Are the two-gNB experiments feasible? Or should they redesign experiments 6-7 to use only ONE gNB?

Let me think about this carefully.
The core issue: Docker Compose depends_on is a startup-ordering mechanism. If UEs depend on gnb2, Compose starts gnb2 automatically. But they want gnb2 to start LATER (after UEs are already registered on gnb1). This is a fundamental conflict with how Compose works.
Can they keep gnb2 in gnbSearchList but NOT in depends_on?
Yes, but with a caveat. depends_on in Docker Compose only affects docker compose up. It does NOT affect runtime behavior. If UEs have gnb2 in their search list but gnb2 is not running, UERANSIM will try gnb1 first (since it's first in the list), and if gnb1 works, they'll camp on gnb1. They won't fail just because gnb2 is unreachable—they'll just not find it and move to the next entry.
Wait, actually, let me reconsider. The user's experience was:

    UEs with gnb2 in search list but NOT in depends_on → "UEs don't start" or fail

But that shouldn't happen if gnb1 is first in the list and running. UERANSIM should try gnb1 first, succeed, and register. The fact that gnb2 is down shouldn't prevent startup.
However, the user said "UEs don't start because they have gNB2 into gnbSearchList and not into depends_on." This might mean:

    The UE containers start but crash because they can't resolve gnb2.ueransim.org (DNS failure)
    OR the user is conflating "container won't start" with "UE process exits"

Actually, if gnb2 container is not running, gnb2.ueransim.org won't resolve in Docker DNS. UERANSIM might handle this gracefully (try next gNB) or it might fail. Looking at earlier logs, when gnb1 was down, UEs showed "Bad Inet address: gnb1.ueransim.org". So UERANSIM does fail hard if a hostname in gnbSearchList doesn't resolve.
So the issue is: if gnb2 is in gnbSearchList but the container isn't running, the UE crashes with DNS resolution failure.
Solutions:

    Use extra_hosts or static IPs instead of Docker DNS aliases - But this is fragile.
    Start gnb2 container but keep it "down" in some way - Not really possible with UERANSIM.
    Use a dummy gnb2 container - Overly complex.
    Redesign experiments 6-7 to use ONE gNB - The user's suggestion. Is this feasible?

Let me think about whether experiments 6-7 can be done with one gNB.
Experiment 6: split-gnb-simultaneous
Original design: AMF switch + gNB2 start + restart second-half UEs all at once, with movers having gnb2 first in search list.
With ONE gNB: Can we simulate "gNB re-selection"? Not really. The whole point is that movers attach to a DIFFERENT gNB. With one gNB, there's no re-selection—just re-registration on the same gNB.
Experiment 7: split-gnb-sequential
Original: AMF switch, wait for stayers to recover on gnb1, then start gnb2 and restart movers.
With ONE gNB: Again, no gNB change possible.
So the experiments fundamentally require two gNBs to study gNB re-selection.
Alternative approach: Can we make the two-gNB setup work?
Yes, but we need to handle the startup sequencing differently. Here are practical options:
Option A: Keep gnb2 in depends_on, but use a "startup script" approach
Instead of trying to prevent gnb2 from starting, let it start. The issue was that gnb2 crashed because proxy2 wasn't ready. But with restart: unless-stopped, gnb2 auto-restarts until proxy2 is ready. The REAL problem was that the AMF got corrupted by the gnb2 crash loop.
Wait, but the user removed restart: unless-stopped based on my earlier advice. Let me reconsider.
Actually, looking back at the conversation:

    gnb2 crashed on startup because proxy2 wasn't ready
    With restart: unless-stopped, it would crash-loop
    The crash-loop corrupted AMF state
    This caused No SMF Instance for ALL UEs

So the problem wasn't just startup timing—it was that gnb2's crash loop poisoned the AMF.
Better approach for two gNBs:

    Start proxy2 FIRST (it's in Phase 1 now, so that's fine)
    Start gnb2 explicitly BEFORE starting UEs, but AFTER the core is stable
    Since UEs depend on gnb2, if gnb2 is already running when UEs start, everything is fine

Wait, but the user wants:

    Phase 1: Core + proxies
    Phase 2: Wait
    Phase 3: gnb1
    Phase 4: UEs (these would auto-start gnb2 if depends_on exists)
    Phase 6: gnb2

This is the conflict. They want gnb2 to start AFTER UEs, but Compose wants it to start WITH UEs.
Solution: Remove depends_on: gnb2 from UEs, but keep gnb2 in gnbSearchList. Handle DNS with a dummy entry or by ensuring gnb2 is started before UEs resolve it.
Actually, the simplest solution is:
Start gnb2 BEFORE UEs, not after.
But the user wants UEs to register on gnb1 FIRST, then later some UEs move to gnb2. If gnb2 is running from the start, UEs might randomly select gnb2 (since both are in the search list). But if gnb1 is listed FIRST, UERANSIM should try gnb1 first and camp there. The issue is whether UERANSIM tries gnb2 at all during initial registration.
Looking at UERANSIM behavior: it tries gNBs in the order of gnbSearchList. If gnb1 succeeds, it stops searching. So if gnb1 is first and working, UEs should all camp on gnb1 even if gnb2 is also running.
So the sequence could be:

    Start core + proxies
    Wait 90s for SMF
    Start gnb1 AND gnb2 simultaneously
    Wait for both to show NG Setup success
    Start UEs (with both gNBs in search list, gnb1 first)
    UEs should all register on gnb1 (since it's first)
    Run experiments

This eliminates the need to start gnb2 after UEs. Both gNBs are up from the beginning, but UEs prefer gnb1 due to list order.
Is there a risk that some UEs pick gnb2?
Only if gnb1 is full or has no capacity. With 6 UEs and UERANSIM's simple cell selection, they should all pick the first working gNB.
What about the original crash-loop issue?
If both gnb1 and gnb2 are started after the core is stable (90s wait), and proxies are already listening, then neither gNB should crash. The startup race is eliminated.
So my recommendation should be:

    Put gnb2 back into all ue*.yaml gnbSearchList (gnb1 first, gnb2 second)
    Keep depends_on: gnb1 only in UEs (remove gnb2 from depends_on)
    In start_network.sh:
        Phase 1: Core + proxies
        Phase 2: Wait 90s
        Phase 3: Start gnb1 AND gnb2 simultaneously
        Phase 4: Wait 5s for both NG Setup
        Phase 5: Start UEs
        UEs will all register on gnb1 (first in list)
    For experiments 6-7: The orchestrator swaps the gnbSearchList for movers, restarts them, and they connect to gnb2

Wait, but the user said: "UEs don't start because they have gNB2 into gnbSearchList and not into depends_on."
If gnb2 container is NOT running, and UEs try to resolve gnb2.ueransim.org, they get DNS failure and crash. This is because Docker's embedded DNS only resolves running containers.
So if we remove depends_on: gnb2, we MUST start gnb2 before starting UEs. We can't start UEs with gnb2 in their config but gnb2 not running.
Alternative: Use extra_hosts to pre-populate gnb2's IP?
No, because Docker assigns IPs dynamically.
Alternative: Start a dummy gnb2 container first?
Too complex.
Best approach: Start gnb2 before UEs, accept that both gNBs are up, and trust that UEs will pick gnb1 due to list order.
But wait—if both gNBs are up and UEs have both in their search list, will UEs split randomly? In UERANSIM, gnbSearchList is tried in order. The UE scans for cells. Each gNB broadcasts a cell. The UE picks the "best" cell. If both cells are equally good (same PLMN, similar signal), UERANSIM might pick based on order or might pick randomly.
Actually, looking at the UERANSIM source code behavior (from the logs we've seen), when there are multiple cells, it says "Selected cell plmn[001/01] tac[1]". It doesn't explicitly say which gNB. But if both gNBs have the same signal strength and the UE tries them in order, it should pick the first one that responds.
However, there's a subtlety: UERANSIM's cell selection is based on RLS (Radio Link Simulation) signal strength, not just list order. If both gNBs are in range and have the same simulated signal strength, the UE might pick either one. But since the list order determines which gNB is tried first during the search phase, and the first successful cell is selected, it should be deterministic.
Actually, I realize there might be a simpler issue: The user might have had depends_on: gnb2 AND gnb2 in the search list, and when they removed depends_on, they didn't realize they needed to start gnb2 manually before UEs. So they started UEs, gnb2 wasn't running, DNS failed, UEs crashed.
My answer should:

    Validate that the two-gNB experiments ARE feasible
    Explain why the startup sequence was the problem, not the concept
    Propose a corrected startup sequence where BOTH gNBs start BEFORE UEs
    Explain that UEs will camp on gnb1 (first in list) even with gnb2 running
    Offer to generate a corrected start_network.sh and compose file guidance

Let me also address the user's suggestion about "reassembly using only one gNB." I should explain that:

    With one gNB, you cannot study gNB re-selection
    The whole point of experiments 6-7 is to measure the cost of moving to a different gNB
    A single-gNB design would only measure AMF reallocation (which is already covered by experiments 1-5)
    So yes, it's technically possible to run "pseudo" experiments 6-7 with one gNB, but they wouldn't measure gNB re-selection latency—they'd just be variations of concurrent/sequential restart

Wait, could we design a meaningful experiment 6-7 with ONE gNB?
Experiment 6 (one-gNB variant): "Simultaneous AMF switch + UE subset restart"

    Switch proxy to amf2
    Restart gnb1
    Restart subset of UEs
    Compare latency of restarted UEs vs non-restarted UEs
    This is basically a variation of "partial" mode with a gNB restart. It measures the cost of restarting UEs after an AMF switch, but not gNB re-selection.

Experiment 7 (one-gNB variant): "Sequential AMF switch + UE subset restart"

    Switch proxy to amf2
    Wait for non-restarted UEs to recover
    Restart subset of UEs
    Again, this is just partial mode with a delay

These are valid experiments but they don't answer the research question about gNB re-selection. The user specifically wanted to study "gNB re-selection" and "gNB re-allocation."

Let me draft the response.
Good to hear from you! The results are solid—your orchestrator is working correctly. Now let me address your strategic question directly:
Yes, the two-gNB experiments are absolutely feasible. The problem you hit was not a conceptual flaw—it was a Docker Compose startup-ordering trap that is easy to fix.
Why the startup sequence failed
Your original start_network.sh tried this:

    Start core
    Start gnb1
    Start UEs (with gnb2 in gnbSearchList but NOT in depends_on)
    Start gnb2 later

The problem: If gnb2 container is not running, gnb2.ueransim.org does not resolve in Docker's embedded DNS. UERANSIM tries to resolve it, gets NXDOMAIN, and crashes immediately with:
plain

Bad Inet address: gnb2.ueransim.org

This is why removing depends_on: gnb2 broke things—you removed the safety net that ensured gnb2 was running before UEs started, but you didn't start gnb2 manually before the UEs.
The fix: start BOTH gNBs BEFORE UEs
UERANSIM tries gNBs in the order of gnbSearchList. If gnb1 is first and working, UEs will camp on gnb1 and never even attempt gnb2. So you can safely have both gNBs running from the start.
Corrected startup sequence:
bash

# Phase 1: Core + proxies
docker compose up -d amf1 amf2 smf1 smf2 upf1 upf2 nrf pcf udr sctp-proxy sctp-proxy2
sleep 90  # Wait for SMF/NRF registration

# Phase 2: Start BOTH gNBs (not just gnb1)
docker compose up -d gnb1 gnb2
sleep 5

# Verify both are healthy
docker logs gnb1 --tail 3 | grep "NG Setup procedure is successful"
docker logs gnb2 --tail 3 | grep "NG Setup procedure is successful"

# Phase 3: Start UEs (both gNBs in search list, gnb1 first)
docker compose up -d ue1 ue2 ue3 ue4 ue5 ue6
# UEs will all register on gnb1 because it's first in the list

In docker-compose.yaml:

    Keep gnb2 OUT of UE depends_on (so docker compose up -d ue1 doesn't auto-start it)
    But make sure your script starts gnb2 explicitly in Phase 2, before UEs

What about the earlier gnb2 crash-loop?
That happened because gnb2 started before proxy2 was ready (the 0.5-second race). With the 90-second wait, proxy2 is fully listening long before gnb2 starts. No crash, no loop, no AMF corruption.
Can you redesign experiments 6–7 with only one gNB?
Technically yes, but they would measure different things:
Table
Experiment	With 2 gNBs	With 1 gNB only
6: Simultaneous	Measures AMF switch + gNB re-selection + concurrent UE restart	Becomes "AMF switch + gNB restart + subset restart"—just a variation of partial mode
7: Sequential	Measures AMF recovery on gnb1, then gNB re-selection to gnb2	Becomes "AMF switch + delayed subset restart"—no actual gNB change
If you drop the second gNB, you lose the ability to measure:

    gNB reselection latency
    Path-divergence offset (gnb1→proxy vs gnb2→proxy)
    Cross-gNB registration offset

You'd just be re-running experiments 1–5 with different timing. So I strongly recommend keeping the two-gNB design.
My recommendation

    Put gnb2 back into all ue*.yaml files (gnb1 first, gnb2 second)
    Keep depends_on: gnb1 only in compose (remove gnb2 from UE depends_on)
    Use the corrected 3-phase startup above (core → both gNBs → UEs)
    Run the dual-proxy orchestrator with --ue-subset ue4,ue5,ue6 for split-gnb modes

The movers will be restarted with gnb2 first in their config, and since gnb2 is already running and healthy, they'll re-select to it cleanly.
Would you like me to generate a corrected start_network.sh and the minimal docker-compose.yaml diff for this approach? Or do you want to try the 3-phase sequence manually first?
